Brauer diagrams, modular operads, and a graphical nerve theorem for circuit algebras Thanks: The author acknowledges the support of Australian Research Council grants DP160101519 and FT160100393.
Sophie Raynor

Abstract
. Circuit algebras, used in the study of finite-type knot invariants, are a symmetric analogue of Jones’s planar algebras. They are very closely related to circuit operads, modular operads equipped with a monoidal product. This paper gives a description of circuit algebras in terms categories of Brauer diagrams, and thereby establishes a link between circuit algebras and the representation theory of classical Lie groups. An abstract nerve theorem for circuit operads – and hence circuit algebras – is proved and a graphical calculus is established using an iterated distributive law, and an existing nerve theorem for modular operads.



Note:
This paper has been superceded by
arXiv:2412.20260
(on circuit algebras, Brauer diagrams, modular operads and invariant theory) and
arXiv:2412.20262
(on a graphical calculus, iterated distributive laws and nerve theorem for circuit algebras). The version below is from November 2022.

1.
Introduction

Circuit algebras are a symmetric (or non-planar) version of Jones’s planar algebras
[
17
]
. They were introduced as a framework for working with virtual tangles in the study of finite type invariants of knotted objects by Bar-Natan and Dansco
[
1
]
. Recently, Dansco, Halacheva and Robertson have shown that oriented circuit algebras are equivalent to wheeled PROPs
[
9
]
, and used this to give descriptions of the (graded) Kashiwara-Vergne group
\mathrm{KV}
(
\mathrm{KRV}
), and the graded Grothendieck-Teichmüller group
\mathrm{GRT}
, as automorphism groups of circuit algebras
[
10
]
.

This paper compares circuit algebras with modular operads in the style of
[
34
]
(see also
[
19
,
14
,
15
]
), and establishes a nerve theorem characterising circuit algebras as presheaves over a category of (disconnected) graphs that satisfy a so-called ‘Segal condition’.
The basic data of a (coloured) circuit algebra is given by a graded monoid
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
, together with a levelwise action of the permutation groupoid, and an additional ‘contraction’ operation that satisfies suitable axioms. By
9.9
, circuit algebras may be viewed as modular operads equipped with an extra external (or monoidal) product. In previous literature
[
9
,
10
,
16
,
43
]
,
circuit algebras have been defined as algebras over an operad of wiring diagrams. The present work describes a family of operads of wiring diagrams in terms of categories of ‘coloured Brauer diagrams’ (or coloured symmetric tangle categories),
and this leads to a new, equivalent definition of a general class of (coloured) circuit algebras:

Theorem 1.1
.

A (coloured) circuit algebra is a symmetric lax monoidal functor from a category of coloured Brauer diagrams.

Versions of the categories of Brauer diagrams in
1.1
have been widely studied for nearly a century since Brauer’s 1937 paper
[
6
]
extending Schur-Weyl duality to describe representations of the finite dimensional orthogonal and symplectic groups (e.g.
[
46
,
24
,
27
]
).
Hence, a pleasing consequence of the approach of this paper is that, via
1.1
(and
9.9
), it establishes a dictionary for translating between categorical (Brauer categories), operadic (circuit algebras) and modular operadic approaches to active research in representation theory and quantum algebra.

The main technical results of the paper are the construction – using an iterated distributive law – of a monad for circuit algebras, and the proof of an abstract ‘Weber-style’
[
45
,
3
]
nerve theorem which provides an explicit description of the graphical combinatorics of circuit algebras. This is closely related to the graphical nerve theorem for modular operads of
[
34
, Theorem 8.2]
and is based on the observation (
9.9
) that, in the category of sets, circuit algebras coincide with modular operads equipped with an extra product operation.

Theorem 1.2

(
9.8
)
.

The category
{\mathsf{CA}}
of set-valued circuit algebras is equivalent to the Eilenberg-Moore (EM) category of algebras for a monad
\mathbb{O}^{\times}
on Joyal and Kock’s graphical species category
\mathsf{GS}

[
19
]
.

As in
[
34
]
, this enables the proof of a corresponding nerve theorem, characterising circuit algebras as functors from a full subcategory
{\Xi^{\times}}\subset{\mathsf{CA}}
whose objects are graphs.
The nerve theorem for circuit algebras is proved using iterated distributive laws
[
8
]
, together with the abstract nerve theory of
[
3
]
. Let
\mathsf{psh}({\Xi^{\times}})
denote the category of presheaves (contravariant
\mathsf{Set}
-valued functors) on
{\Xi^{\times}}
, then:

Theorem 1.3

(
10.4
)
.

The nerve functor
N\colon{\mathsf{CA}}\to\mathsf{psh}({\Xi^{\times}})
induced by the inclusion
{\Xi^{\times}}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{CA}}
is fully faithful.
Moreover, there is a canonical restriction functor
R^{*}\colon\mathsf{psh}({\Xi^{\times}})\to\mathsf{GS}
, such that a presheaf
P
on
{\Xi^{\times}}
is equivalent to the nerve of a circuit algebra if and only if
P
is completely described by
R^{*}P
, in which case
P
is said to ‘be Segal’, or ‘satisfy the Segal condition’.

Before outlining the construction, let us consider some reasons why such a result may be of interest.

As has already been mentioned, the description of circuit algebras in terms of categories of Brauer diagrams indicates a fundamental relationship between circuit algebras, (topological) field theories, and representations of diverse algebraic structures – including the classical Lie groups – of interest in mathematics and physics.

1.1
enables us to translate circuit algebra and modular operad machinery to other (algebraic) contexts. Moreover, Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
, establish a new graphical calculus for studying representations of Brauer categories.

In
[
34
, Section 6]
, I explained how the so-called ‘problem of loops’ creates an obstacle to constructing the modular operad monad on
\mathsf{GS}
.
This is also an issue for the circuit algebra monad.
In each case, the problem is resolved using distributive laws
[
2
]
. See
[
34
, Section 7]
for the construction of the modular operad monad.
Section

9.2
describes the construction of the circuit algebra monad. For circuit algebras, it is even necessary to construct a triple of monads, and an iterated distributive law
[
8
]
.

Theorem 1.4

(
9.7
&
9.8
)
.

There is a triple of monads
\mathbb{L},\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{T}
on
\mathsf{GS}
and distributive laws between them that
describe an iterated distributive law
[
8
]
. The EM category of algebras for the induced composite monad
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
is equivalent (via
9.9
) to the category
{\mathsf{CA}}
of (
\mathsf{Set}
-valued) circuit algebras.

Here, the composite
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
is the modular operad monad on
\mathsf{GS}
described in
[
34
, Section 7]
, and
\mathbb{T}
is the non-unital modular operad monad of
[
34
, Section 5]
. Hence
1.4
implies that circuit algebras are, equivalently, modular operads with extra structure.

By
7.16
and
9.4
, algebras for the monad
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{T}
are non-unital circuit algebras, whose underlying modular operadic multiplication is not required to have a multiplicative unit. And, by
Section

7
these
are just symmetric monoidal functors from subcategories of ‘
downward
’ Brauer diagrams
[
38
,
25
]
. Hence, it follows immediately from
Section

7
and
[
38
, Sections 3 & 4]
, that the
categories of representations of the infinite orthogonal and symplectic groups (respectively the infinite general linear group) are canonically described by full subcategories of non-unital monochrome circuit algebras (respectively non-unital monochrome oriented circuit algebras). A similar idea has been exploited by
Kupers and Randall-Williams in
[
25
]
to prove that the Malcev Lie algebras associated to the Torelli groups of surfaces of arbitrary genus are stably Koszul. The same observation appears in
[
42
]
where modular operads are obtained as lax functors from the
Brauer properad
.

Unital circuit algebras are more complicated due to the problem of loops
[
34
]
.
1.1
identifies representations of so-called Brauer categories – in the sense of
[
27
]
, and recently studied in e.g
[
36
,
37
,
39
]
– with circuit algebras satisfying additional dimension and symmetry constraints: for example, there are circuit algebras describing sequences of representations of finite dimensional orthogonal groups. It is an ongoing project, with R. Street, to further investigate this approach to representations of Brauer (and related) categories.

Weakening the axioms to consider
(\infty,1)
or
up-to-homotopy
circuit algebras, provides a second motivation for results of this paper. This is relevant, for example, to the work of Dansco, Halacheva and Robertson comparing the Grothendieck-Teichmüller and Kashiwara-Verne groups
\mathrm{GT}
and
\mathrm{KV}
. In
[
10
]
, they have used circuit algebras to obtain results relating the
graded
Grothendieck-Teichmüller and Kashiwara-Verne groups
\mathrm{GRT}
and
\mathrm{KRV}
. However, under passage to pro-unipotent completion, the circuit algebra operations are not preserved on the nose. Hence, to obtain an ungraded version of these results relating
\mathrm{GT}
and
\mathrm{KV}
directly, it is necessary to adopt a weakened notion of circuit algebras (see
[
10
, Introduction, Remark 1.1]
). Since
1.3
gives a description of (
\mathsf{Set}
-valued) circuit algebras in terms of a
strong
Segal condition, it is natural to define
(\infty,1)
-circuit algebras as 9simplicial set valued) presheaves that satisfy a weakened Segal condition. Indeed,
[
15
]
and
[
34
]
proved – via different methods, and using slightly different graphical categories – that
weakening the modular operadic Segal condition gives a reasonable notion of
(\infty,1)
-modular operads, that corresponds with the fibrant objects in a model structure on the relevant category of graphical presheaves. Unfortunately, neither of these models generalises immediately to circuit algebras. Nonetheless, one hopes that the characterisations of circuit algebras, both as algebras of operads of wiring diagrams, and in terms of algebras for the monad
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
, should indicate how to transfer an existing model structure on subcategory of
dendroidal sets
to obtain a model on
{\Xi^{\times}}
-spaces, and thereby obtain
(\infty,1)
-circuit algebras in terms of the expected weak version of the Segal condition in
1.3
.

Finally, it is worth highlighting the abstract machinery of this paper – specifically the combined application of (iterated) distributive laws and abstract nerve theory – that extends those of
[
34
]
. The monad for (set-valued) circuit algebras is constructed in
Section

9
as a composition – via an iterated distributive law
[
8
]
– of three monads, each of which governs a different aspect of the structure of circuit algebras. The proof of
1.3
uses the abstract nerve theory of
[
3
]
, but (as with
[
34
, Theorem 8.2]
) essentially depends on the decomposition of the circuit algebra monad into its constituent parts. The same technique is used again in
[
35
]
(in preparation).
An accessible account of the interplay of abstract nerve theory and distributive laws is given in
Section

2
. It would be interesting to explore if this strategy of using distributive laws to broaden the scope for applying abstract nerve theory has wider applications.

1.1.
Details and overview

In
[
34
]
, it was shown that modular operads are algebras for a monad on a certain combinatorial category
\mathsf{GS}
whose objects are coloured collections called ‘graphical species’
[
19
]
, and a graphical category and corresponding nerve theorem for modular operads were obtained, using the nerve machinery of
[
3
]
.

This paper builds on that result by comparing circuit algebras with graded monoids in the category of modular operads, here called ‘circuit operads’.
It will be proved in
9.9
that, in the category of sets, circuit algebras and circuit operads are equivalent.
More generally, circuit algebras with values in an arbitrary symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
are
enrichments
of
\mathsf{Set}
-valued circuit algebras, with a set (or palette) of objects (colours), and a set of
\mathcal{V}
-objects encoding (multi-)morphisms between them.
By contrast, circuit operads satisfy the same axioms, but
internal
to a category
\mathsf{E}
with finite limits. Hence circuit operads in
\mathsf{E}
are described by an
object object
,
and a
graded (multi)-morphism object
in
\mathsf{E}
.

The proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
relies on an iterated distributive law and the abstract nerve machinery of
[
3
,
5
]
.
Section

2
provides a relatively informal and accessible introduction to these notions.

After establishing notation and terminology for symmetric monoidal categories,
Section

3
provides a detailed discussion of the categories of (coloured) Brauer diagrams and their functors. These are used, in
Section

4
, to define circuit algebras.

Section

4.1
provides a quick introduction to operads and their algebras. Operads of wiring diagrams and circuit algebras are introduced in
Section

4.2
.

In
Section

5
, circuit operads are introduced as presheaves over the category
\mathsf{GS}
of graphical species
[
19
]
. A canonical functor from circuit algebras in
\mathsf{Set}
to circuit operads is described, as well as a monadic adjunction between circuit operads and modular operads (as they are defined in
[
34
]
).

The technical work of the paper starts at
Section

6
. This section is largely a review of basic graph constructions, based, as in
[
34
]
(and
[
14
,
15
]
), on the definition of graphs introduced in
[
19
]
.

A monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
for non-unital circuit operads is constructed in
Section

7
. This is almost identical to the monad discussed by Kock in
[
23
]
and very similar to the monad
\mathbb{T}
for non-unital modular operads constructed in
[
34
]
.
Section

8
reviews (and extends) the construction in
[
34
]
of the modular operad monad
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
by composing
\mathbb{T}
with a unit monad
\mathbb{D}
.

However, in spite of the close similarities between
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
and
\mathbb{T}
, it is not possible to obtain the monad for unital circuit operads directly from a distributive law involving
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
.
Instead, it is necessary to supplement the construction of
[
34
]
by observing that
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
is, itself a composite monad.

This happens in
Section

9
, in which an iterated distributive law is described and it is shown that the resulting composite
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
of three monads on
\mathsf{GS}
is indeed the desired circuit operad monad. Moreover, it is proved that the forgetful functor from
\mathsf{Set}
-valued circuit algebras to
\mathsf{Set}
-valued circuit operads is, in fact, an equivalence of categories.

Finally,
Section

10
contains a description of the graphical category
{\Xi^{\times}}
for circuit operads. From there, the proof of the nerve theorem (
1.3
) is straightforward.
Let
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
be the EM category of algebras for the monad
\mathbb{D}
on
\mathsf{GS}
. By the classical theory of distributive laws
[
2
]
, there is a lifted monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{T}_{*}
on
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
whose algebras are circuit operads.
1.3
is then implied by the observation that
the lifted monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{T}_{*}
on
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}

satisfies the conditions of the abstract nerve machinery of
[
3
]
: it has ‘arities’ (see
Section

2.3
).

2.
Distributive laws and abstract nerve theory

The main result of this work is the construction of a monad (
9.8
), and a graphical category and nerve theorem (
10.4
) for circuit algebras that give a complete description of their combinatorics. The method uses the machinery of abstract nerve theory
[
3
,
45
]
, in combination with (iterated) distributive laws
[
2
,
8
]
.

Given its connections with representations of classical groups, this work is aimed at a diverse audience including representation theorists and topologists, as well as category theorists. Therefore, this section is intended to
provide an accessible explanation of these formal concepts and how they combine to prove
10.4
.

2.1.
Iterated distributive laws

Informally, monads are gadgets that encode, via their algebras, (algebraic) structure on objects. The key principle underlying this work is that it is possible to build up a description of more complicated algebraic structures on the objects of some category by suitably ‘combining’ monads that describe simpler structures. To do this, we need distributive laws
[
2
]
.

For a more detailed review of the general theory of monads and their algebras, see for example
[
30
, Chapter VI]
. For now, it suffices that a monad
\mathbb{M}=(M,\mu^{\mathbb{M}},\eta^{\mathbb{M}})
on a category
\mathsf{C}
is given by an endofunctor
M\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
, together with natural transformations
\mu^{\mathbb{M}}\colon M^{2}\Rightarrow M
(called the
monadic multiplication
), and
\eta^{\mathbb{M}}\colon\mathrm{id}_{\mathsf{Cat}}\Rightarrow M
(called the
monadic unit
), that satisfy certain axioms making
\mathbb{M}
into an associative monoid in the category of endofunctors on
\mathsf{C}
.

An algebra
(x,\theta)
for
\mathbb{M}
consists of an object
x
of
\mathsf{C}
, and a morphism
\theta\colon Mx\to x
that satisfies two axioms that assert the compatibility of
\theta
with
\mu^{\mathbb{M}}
and
\eta^{\mathbb{M}}
. Algebras are the objects of the
Eilenberg-Moore (EM) category of algebras for
\mathbb{M}
, whose morphisms
(x,\theta)\to(y,\rho)
are given by morphisms
f\in\mathsf{C}(x,y)
such that
\rho\circ Mf=f\circ\theta
.

In general, monads do not compose, since the data of a pair of monads
\mathbb{M}=(M,\mu^{\mathbb{M}},\eta^{\mathbb{M}})
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}=({M^{\prime}},\mu^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}},\eta^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}})
on a category
\mathsf{C}
is not sufficient to define a multiplication for the composite endofunctor
MM^{\prime}\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}
.

Observe however that, given any natural transformation
\lambda\colon{M^{\prime}}M\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
, there is an induced natural transformation
\mu_{\lambda}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(\mu^{\mathbb{M}}\mu^{\mathbb{{M^{\prime}}}})\circ(M\lambda M^{\prime})\colon(M{M^{\prime}})^{2}\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
distributive law
for
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
is a natural transformation
\lambda\colon{M^{\prime}}M\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
such that the triple
(M{M^{\prime}},\mu_{\lambda},\eta^{\mathbb{M}}\eta^{\mathbb{{M^{\prime}}}})
defines a monad
\mathbb{M}\mathbb{{M^{\prime}}}
on
\mathsf{C}
.

Distributive laws were first introduced by Beck
[
2
]
and are characterised by four axioms that
determine how the algebraic structures encoded by the separate monads
interact with each other to form the structure encoded by their composite.

Example 2.2
.

The category of small categories is isomorphic to the EM category of algebras for the category monad on the category of directed graphs. This monad sends a directed graph
G
to the graph
\widetilde{G}
with the same vertices, and a directed edge
v\leadsto w
for each directed path (of length
n\geq 0
) from
v
to
w
in
G
.

This monad may be obtained as a composite of the
semi-category monad
– which governs associative composition and assigns to each directed graph
G
the graph with the same vertices as
G
and edges corresponding to non-trivial directed paths (of length
n>0
) in
G
– and the
reflexive graph monad
that adjoins a distinguished loop at each vertex of a directed graph.
The corresponding distributive law encodes the property that the adjoined loops provide identities for the (semi-categorical) composition.

Example 2.3
.

In
[
34
]
, the monad for modular operads on the category
\mathsf{GS}
of graphical species (
5.3
) is obtained, as in
2.2
for categories, as a composite
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
: the monad
\mathbb{T}
governs the composition structure (multiplication and contraction), and the monad
\mathbb{D}
adjoins distinguished elements that encode the combinatorics of the multiplicative unit. The distributive law ensures that the distinguished elements provide units for the multiplication (see also
Section

8
).

As usual, let
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
denote the EM category of algebras for a monad
\mathbb{M}
on
\mathsf{C}
.

Given monads
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{C}
, and a distributive law
\lambda\colon{M^{\prime}}M\Rightarrow M{M^{\prime}}
, there is, by
[
2
, Section 3]
, a commuting square of strict monadic adjunctions:

(2.4)

\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}\mathbb{M}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Moreover, by
[
8
]
, the composite
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}
of an
n
-tuple of monads
\mathbb{M}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{M}_{n}
on
\mathsf{C}
exists if there are pairwise distributive laws
\lambda_{i,j}:M_{i}M_{j}\Rightarrow M_{j}M_{i}
, for
1\leq i<j\leq n
such that for each triple
1\leq i<j<k\leq n
, the corresponding triple of monads and distributive laws satisfies (Yang-Baxter) conditions described in (
9.1
). In this case, there is an
n
-cube of adjunctions
as illustrated in (
2.5
) for the case
n=3
(where only the left adjoints are marked):

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{3}\mathbb{M}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{3}\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{3}\mathbb{M}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{2}\mathbb{M}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}}

2.2.
Presheaves and slice categories

Before discussing nerve functors, and the abstract nerve theory of
[
3
,
45
]
, it is first necessary to review (and establish notation for) certain categories of presheaves.

So, let
\mathsf{E}
be a category (with finite limits), and let
\mathsf{C}
be an essentially small category. An
\mathsf{E}
-valued
\mathsf{C}
-presheaf
is a functor
S\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{E}
. The corresponding functor category is denoted by
\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{E}}}(\mathsf{C})
. If
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Set}
, the category of sets and all set maps, then
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{C})\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{E}}}(\mathsf{C})
.

When
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Set}
, the category of elements (or
Grothendieck construction
) of a presheaf
P\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
is defined as follows:

Definition 2.6
.

Objects of the category
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)
of elements of a presheaf
P\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
are pairs
(c,x)
– called
elements of
P
– where
c
is an object of
\mathsf{C}
and
x\in P(c)
. Morphisms
(c,x)\to(d,y)
in
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)
are given by morphisms
f\in\mathsf{C}(c,d)
such that
P(f)(y)=x
.

Recall that, if a presheaf
P\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
is representable (i.e.
P=\mathsf{C}(-,c)
, for some
c\in\mathsf{C}
), then
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)
is precisely the
slice category

\mathsf{C}/c
whose objects are pairs
(d,f)
where
f\in\mathsf{C}(d,c)
, and morphisms
(d,f)\to(d^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are commuting triangles in
\mathsf{C}
:

\textstyle{d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{d^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{c}

In general, a functor
\iota\colon\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
induces a presheaf
\iota^{*}\mathsf{C}(-,c)
,
d\mapsto\mathsf{C}(\iota(d),c)
on
\mathsf{D}
. For all
c\in\mathsf{C}
, the category
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{D}}(\iota^{*}\mathsf{C}(-,c))
is called the
slice category of
\mathsf{D}
over
c
and is denoted by
\iota/c
, or simply
\mathsf{D}/c
.

The
slice category of
c
over
\mathsf{D}
, denoted by
c/\mathsf{D}
(or
c/\iota
) is the category
\iota^{\mathrm{op}}/c
whose objects are morphisms
f\in\mathsf{C}(c,\iota(-))
and whose morphisms
(d,f)\to(d^{\prime},f)
are given by
g\in\mathsf{D}(d,d^{\prime})
such that
\iota(g)\circ g=f^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}(c,\iota(d^{\prime}))
.

Example 2.7
.

Let
\iota\colon\mathsf{D}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}
be an inclusion of small categories. By
[
26
, Proposition 5.1]
, the induced nerve
N\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D})
if fully faithful if and only if every object
c\in\mathsf{C}
is canonically the colimit of the forgetful functor
\mathsf{D}/c\to\mathsf{C}
,
(f\colon\iota(d)\to c)\mapsto\iota(d)
. In this case, the inclusion
\iota\colon\mathsf{D}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}
is called
dense
.

Observe, in particular, that the Yoneda embedding
\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{C})
induces a canonical isomorphism
\mathsf{el}_{\mathsf{C}}(P)\cong\mathsf{C}/P
for all presheaves
P\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Set}
. These categories will be identified in this work.

2.3.
Abstract nerve theory

A functor
F\colon\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{C}
(between ordinary categories) induces a
nerve functor

N_{\mathsf{D}}\colon\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D})
given by
N_{\mathsf{D}}(c)(d)=\mathsf{C}(Fd,c)
for all
c\in\mathsf{C}
and
d\in\mathsf{D}
.

Example 2.8
.

The nerve of a small category
\mathsf{C}
is described by the image of
\mathsf{C}
under the nerve functor induced by the inclusion
\Delta\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Cat}
, where
\mathsf{Cat}
is the category of small categories, and
\Delta
is the (topological)
simplex category
of non-empty finite ordinals and order preserving morphisms.

Recall that every functor
admits an (up to isomorphism) unique
bo-ff factorisation
as a bijective on objects functor followed by a fully faithful functor. For example, if
\mathbb{M}
is a monad on a category
\mathsf{C}
, and
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
is the EM category of algebras for
\mathbb{M}
, then the free functor
\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
has bo-ff factorisation
\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{\mathbb{M}}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
, where
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbb{M}}
is the Kleisli category of free
\mathbb{M}
-algebras (see e.g.
[
30
, Section VI.5]
).

Hence, for any subcategory
\mathsf{D}
of
\mathsf{C}
, the bo-ff factorisation of the canonical functor
\mathsf{D}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{C}\xrightarrow{\text{ free}}\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
factors through the full subcategory
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
of
\mathsf{C}_{\mathbb{M}}
with objects from
\mathsf{D}
. There is
an induced diagram of functors

(2.9)

\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathsf{D}}}
free
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}).}

where
j^{*}
is the pullback of the bijective on objects functor
j\colon\mathsf{D}\to\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
. The left square of (
2.9
) commutes by definition, and the right square commutes up to natural isomorphism.

By construction, the defining functor
\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\to\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
is fully faithful. Abstract nerve theory describes conditions on
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathbb{M}
that ensure that the induced nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
is also fully faithful
[
3
,
45
]
. If there is a subcategory
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
such that
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathsf{D}
satisfy these conditions, then
\mathbb{M}
is called a
monad with arities
, and
\mathsf{D}

provides arities for
\mathbb{M}
.

Moreover, by
[
45
, Section 4]
, if
\mathsf{D}
provides arities for
\mathbb{M}
, then objects in the image of
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}
in
\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
are (up to equivalence) precisely those that are mapped by
j^{*}
to the image of
N_{\mathsf{D}}
in
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D})
.
This is called the
Segal condition
for the nerve theorem.

2.4.
Using distributive laws to prove nerve theorems

Now, let
\mathsf{D}\subset\mathsf{C}
be a dense subcategory of an essentially small category, and let
\mathbb{M}
be a monad on
\mathsf{C}
.

Even if
\mathbb{M}
and
\mathsf{D}
do not satisfy the conditions of
[
3
, Sections 1 & 2]
, if
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{n}\dots\mathbb{M}^{1}
is a composite monad, then it may still be possible to use abstract nerve theory, together with the distributive laws for the composite
\mathbb{M}^{n}\dots\mathbb{M}^{1}
, to establish that the induced nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
is fully faithful.

Namely, the existence of pairwise distributive laws means that each of the
n!
paths of left adjoints from
\mathsf{C}
to
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}
in the cube (
2.5
) describes a distinct factorisation of the diagram (
2.9
) into
n
stages. The nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\colon\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})
is fully faithful if the conditions of
[
3
]
hold for any of these stages.

For example, if
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}^{n}\dots\mathbb{M}^{1}
, then there is a factorisation of the diagram (
2.9
) as

(2.10)

\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{n}^{*}}
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{n}}
b.o.
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
free
\textstyle{\vdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
forget
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{1}}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\scriptstyle{N_{\mathsf{D}}}
free
\textstyle{\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{D}).}

Hence, to prove that the induced nerve
N_{\mathbb{M},\mathsf{D}}=N_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1},\mathsf{D}}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}}

is fully faithful, it is sufficient to consider whether there is an
1<i<n
such that the conditions of
[
3
]
are satisfied by
\Theta_{\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i},\mathsf{D}}

together with the lift of the monad
\mathbb{M}_{n}\dots\mathbb{M}_{i+1}
on
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{M}_{i}\dots\mathbb{M}_{1}}
. The same trick can be applied for every other path in the cube (
2.5
).

This is the method used in
Section

10
to prove
10.4
, the nerve theorem for circuit operads: In
Section

9.2
, the monad for circuit operads is described as a composite
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
of three monads defined on the category
\mathsf{GS}
of graphical species. On
\mathsf{GS}
, the composite
\mathbb{L}\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}
does not satisfy the conditions of
[
3
]
. However, the induced monad
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{T}_{*}
on the category
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
of
\mathbb{D}
-algebras does. Hence, there is a full subcategory
{\Xi^{\times}}\subset\mathsf{CO}
of the category of circuit operads that induces a fully faithful nerve functor
\mathsf{CO}\to\mathsf{psh}({\Xi^{\times}})
(
10.4
).

3.
Brauer diagrams

In
Section

4
, circuit algebras will be defined in terms of Brauer diagrams. These are an important tool in the representation theory of orthogonal, symplectic and general linear groups
[
6
,
39
]
.

The category
\mathsf{BD}
of monochrome Brauer diagrams is introduced in
Section

3.2
. This definition is generalised to the categories
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
of coloured Brauer diagrams in
Section

3.3
.

Before defining these categories,
Section

3.1
provides a brief outline of the notation and terminology conventions for symmetric monoidal categories
that will be used in the rest of the paper.

3.1.
Symmetric monoidal categories and duality

For precise definitions and a detailed discussion, see e.g.
[
11
, Chapter 8]
.

Recall that a
monoidal category
is a category
\mathcal{V}
together with a bifunctor
\otimes\colon\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}
(the
monoidal product
) that is associative up to natural
associator
isomorphism, and for which there is an object
I
of
\mathcal{V}
(the
monoidal unit
) that acts as a two-sided identity for
\otimes
up to natural (left and right)
unitor
isomorphisms. The monoidal product and the associator and unitor isomorphisms are required to satisfy axioms that mean that
certain sensible diagrams commute. A monoidal category is called
strict monoidal
if the associator and unitor isomorphisms are the identity.

A monoidal structure on
\mathcal{V}
is
braided
if,
for all objects
x,y\in\mathcal{V}
, there are isomorphisms
\sigma_{x,y}\colon x\otimes y\rightarrow y\otimes x
that satisfy the familiar braid
identities

(3.1)

(\sigma_{y,z}\otimes id_{x})(id_{y}\otimes\sigma_{x,z})(\sigma_{x,y}\otimes id_{z})=(id_{z}\otimes\sigma_{x,y})(\sigma_{x,z}\otimes id_{y})(id_{x}\otimes\sigma_{y,z})\ \text{ for all }x,y,z.

If
\sigma_{y,x}=\sigma_{x,y}^{-1}
for all
x,y
, then the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{V}
is
symmetric
.

Remark 3.2
.

In this paper, the associators, unitors and symmetry isomorphisms will be ignored in the notation, and (symmetric) monoidal categories will be denoted simply by
\mathcal{V}
, or sometimes
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
.

Symmetric strict monoidal categories are called
permutative categories
. The notation
\oplus
and
0
will often be used to designate the monoidal product and unit of a permutative category
(\mathsf{C},\oplus,0)
.

A
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured PROP
is a small permutative category
\mathsf{C}
whose object monoid is free on a set
\mathfrak{D}
. When
\mathfrak{D}=\{1\}
is a singleton, then
\mathsf{C}
is a (monochrome) PROP in the original sense of
[
29
]
.

A
(lax) monoidal functor

(\mathcal{V}_{1},\otimes_{1},I_{1})\to(\mathcal{V}_{2},\otimes_{2},I_{2})
is described by a functor

\Theta\colon\mathcal{V}_{1}\to\mathcal{V}_{2}
, together with a morphism
\eta_{\Theta}\colon I_{2}\rightarrow\Theta(I_{1})
in
\mathcal{V}_{2}
and a natural transformation
\theta\colon\Theta(-)\otimes_{2}\Theta(-)\Rightarrow\Theta(-\otimes_{1}-)
such that all the expected structure diagrams commute. A monoidal functor
(\Theta,\theta,\eta_{\Theta})
is called
strong
if
\theta
and
\eta_{\Theta}
are invertible, and
strict
if they are the identity. For convenience,
\eta_{\Theta}
, and
\theta
will usually be suppressed in the notation.

Symmetric monoidal categories form a category
{\mathsf{Sym}}
whose
morphisms
\Theta\colon(\mathcal{V}_{1},\otimes_{1},I_{1})\to(\mathcal{V}_{2},\otimes_{2},I_{2})

are monoidal functors that preserve the symmetry isomorphisms strictly.

Example 3.3
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathbf{n}
denote the set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
(so
\mathbf{0}=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
), and let
\Sigma_{n}
be the group of permutations on
\mathbf{n}
.

Let
\Sigma
be the
symmetric groupoid
with
\Sigma(n,n)=\Sigma_{n}
for all
n
, and
\Sigma(m,n)=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
when
m\neq n
. There is a permutative structure on
\Sigma
induced by addition of natural numbers. For any symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
and any choice of object
x\in\mathcal{V}
, there is a unique symmetric strict monoidal functor
\Sigma\to\mathcal{V}
with
0\mapsto I
and
1\mapsto x
.

An object
x
of a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
has a dual object
x^{*}
in
\mathcal{V}
if there are morphisms
\cap^{\mathsf{C}}_{x}\colon I\to x\otimes x^{*}
,
\cup^{\mathsf{C}}_{x}\colon x^{*}\otimes x\to I
such that the
triangle identities
hold:

(3.4)

(\cup^{\mathsf{C}}_{x}\otimes id_{x})\circ(id_{x}\otimes\cap^{\mathsf{C}}_{x})=id_{x}=(id_{x}\otimes\cup^{\mathsf{C}}_{x^{*}})\circ(\cap^{\mathsf{C}}_{x^{*}}\otimes id_{x}).

.

Figure 1.
The triangle identities.

Definition 3.5
.

A
compact closed category
is a symmetric monoidal category such that every object has a dual
[
29
]
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a compact closed category. For all morphisms
f\in\mathcal{V}(x,y)
, there is a corresponding
evaluation morphism

{\lfloor{f}\rfloor}\in\mathcal{V}(y^{*}\otimes x,I)
induced by composition with composition with
\cup_{y}
(
Figure

2
(a)), and
coevaluation morphism

{\lceil{f}\rceil}\in\mathcal{V}(I,y\otimes x^{*})
induced by composition with
\cap_{x}
(
Figure

2
(b)):

(3.6)

{\lfloor{f}\rfloor}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\cup_{y}\circ(id_{y}\otimes f)\text{ and }{\lceil{f}\rceil}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(f\otimes id_{x})\circ\cap_{x}.

And there is a
dual morphism

f^{*}\in\mathcal{V}(y^{*},x^{*})
(
Figure

2
(c))
given by

(3.7)

f^{*}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(\cup_{y}\otimes id_{x})\circ(id_{y}\otimes f\otimes id_{x})\circ(id_{y}\otimes\cap_{x}).

(The dual morphism
f^{*}
is called the
transpose morphism
in
[
40
]
.)

(a)
id_{y}
f
(b)
f
id_{x}
(c)
id_{y}
f
id_{x}

Figure 2.
(a)
{\lfloor{f}\rfloor}\colon y^{*}\otimes x\to I
; (b)
{\lceil{f}\rceil}\colon I\to y\otimes x^{*}
; (c)
f^{*}\colon y^{*}\to x^{*}
.

For composable morphisms
f
and
g
,
(g\circ f)^{*}=f^{*}\circ g^{*}
in
\mathcal{V}
.

3.2.
Monochrome Brauer diagrams

The category
\mathsf{BD}
of monochrome Brauer diagrams admits a concise definition as the free compact closed category generated by a single self-dual object. This section gives a more concrete description of
\mathsf{BD}
in terms of pairings on finite sets.

Definition 3.8
.

A
pairing
(or
perfect matching
)
\tau
on a set
X
is a partition of
X
into two-element subsets.

Equivalently, a pairing on
X
is a fixed point free involution
\tau\colon X\to X
:

\tau^{2}=id_{X},\text{ and }\tau(x)\neq x\text{ for all }x\in X.

In particular, a finite set
X
admits a pairing if and only if it has even cardinality. The empty set has a unique trivial pairing
\varnothing
by convention.

Example 3.9
.

The boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
of a compact 1-manifold
\mathcal{M}
has a canonical pairing
\tau^{\mathcal{M}}
, with
x=\tau^{\mathcal{M}}y
if
x,y\in\partial\mathcal{M}
,
x\neq y
, are in the same connected component of
\mathcal{M}
.

If
X
and
Y
are finite sets, a pairing
\tau
on their disjoint union
X\amalg Y
may be represented by a graph whose vertices are indexed by
X\amalg Y
, with elements of
X
above those of
Y
, and edges connecting vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
if and only if the corresponding elements of
X\amalg Y
are identified by
\tau
.

Definition 3.10
.

A
(monochrome) Brauer diagram

f
between natural numbers
m
and
n
is given by a pair
(\tau_{f},\mathrm{k}_{f})
of
a pairing
\tau
on the disjoint union
\{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}\amalg\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
, and a natural number
\mathrm{k}_{f}
called the
number of closed components of
f
.

If
\mathrm{k}_{f}=0
, then
f
is called an
open Brauer diagram
.

Let
\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
denote the set of Brauer diagrams from
m
to
n
and let
\mathring{\mathsf{BD}}(m,n)\subset\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
be the subset of open Brauer diagrams. So
\mathsf{BD}(m,n)\cong\mathring{\mathsf{BD}}(m,n)\times\mathbb{N}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Example 3.11
.

For all
n
, there is a canonical inclusion
\Sigma_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathring{\mathsf{BD}}(n,n)
that takes
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
to the open Brauer diagram induced by the pairing
s_{i}\mapsto t_{\sigma i}
on
\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}\amalg\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
,
1\leq i\leq n
.

In particular, the pairing
s_{i}\mapsto t_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
defines the
identity (open) Brauer diagram

id_{n}
on
n
.

Brauer diagrams
(\tau_{1},\mathrm{k}_{1})\colon m_{1}\to n_{1}
and
(\tau_{2},\mathrm{k}_{2})\colon m_{2}\to n_{2}
compose
horizontally
according to

(\tau_{1},\mathrm{k}_{1})\oplus(\tau_{2},\mathrm{k}_{2})=(\tau_{1}\amalg\tau_{2},\mathrm{k}_{1}+\mathrm{k}_{2})\colon m_{1}+m_{2}\to n_{1}+n_{2},

and the trivial open Brauer diagram
(\varnothing,0)\colon 0\to 0
acts as an identity for this composition.

Any Brauer diagram
f=(\tau,\mathrm{k})\colon m\to n
may be written as a horizontal sum
(\tau,0)\oplus(\varnothing,\mathrm{k})
of an open Brauer diagram
(\tau,0)\colon m\to n
and a scalar
(\varnothing,\mathrm{k})=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\mathrm{k}}(\varnothing,1)\colon 0\to 0
.

if the corresponding elements of
X\amalg Y
are equivalent under
\tau
.

A Brauer diagram
f=(\tau,\mathrm{k})\colon m\to n
may be represented by the graph for
\tau
, together with

\mathrm{k}
closed circles (called
bubbles
in
[
37
]
) drawn next to the graph. Horizontal composition of Brauer diagrams is represented by juxtaposition of graphs and closed bubbles.

To define vertical composition of Brauer diagrams, observe that, given finite sets
X,Y,Z
, and pairings
\tau_{X,Y}
and
\tau_{Y,Z}
on
\mathcal{X}\amalg Y
and
Y\amalg Z
respectively, there is an induced pairing
\tau_{Y,Z}\circ\tau_{X,Y}
on
X\amalg Z
obtained, as in
Figure

3
, by stacking
\tau_{X,Y}
and
\tau_{Y,Z}
:

Namely,

\tau_{X,Y}
and
\tau_{Y,Z}
generate an equivalence relation on
X\amalg Y\amalg Z

such that objects
x
and
y
are equivalent if and only if they are related by a sequence of (alternating) applications of
\tau_{X,Y}
and
\tau_{Y,Z}

(
Figure

3
(b)(i)-(iv)).

Each equivalence class contains precisely zero or two elements of
X\amalg Z
. The classes that contain two elements of
X\amalg Z

– the
open components
of the composition – describe the desired pairing
\tau_{Y,Z}\circ\tau_{X,Y}
on
X\amalg Z
.

The remaining equivalence classes – that describe cycles of elements of
Y
– are called
closed components formed by the composition
of
\tau_{X,Y}
and
\tau_{Y,Z}
.

Write
\mathfrak{S}(f)=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}
for the
source
, and
\mathfrak{T}(f)=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
for the
target
of a Brauer diagram
f\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
.
Brauer diagrams
f=(\tau_{f},\mathrm{k}_{f})\in\mathsf{BD}(l,m)
and
g=(\tau_{g},\mathrm{k}_{g})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
may be composed vertically to obtain a Brauer diagram
g\circ f=(\tau_{gf},\mathrm{k}_{gf})\in\mathsf{BD}(l,n)
. The pairing
\tau_{g,f}
is the composition pairing
\tau g\circ\tau f
obtained by identifying
\mathfrak{T}(f)=\mathfrak{S}(g)
according to
t^{f}_{i}\mapsto s^{g}_{i}
. The natural number
\mathrm{k}_{gf}
satisfies

\mathrm{k}_{gf}=\mathrm{k}_{f}+\mathrm{k}_{g}\ +\mathrm{k}(\tau_{f},\tau_{g})

where
\mathrm{k}(\tau_{f},\tau_{g})
is the number of closed components formed by the composition of
\tau_{f}
and
\tau_{g}
.

(a)
X
Y
Y
Z
X
Y
Z
(b)

Figure 3.
(a) Composition of pairings on
X\amalg Y
and
Y\amalg Z
; (c) the resulting pairing on
X\amalg Z
, together with the single closed component formed in the composition.

This composition is associative, with two-sided unit
(id_{n},0)
. Hence, we may define:

Definition 3.12
.

Objects of the category
\mathsf{BD}
of
(monochrome) Brauer diagrams
are natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Morphisms in

\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
are Brauer diagrams
f\colon m\to n
, and composition is vertical composition of Brauer diagrams.

The category
\mathsf{BD}
is a (monochrome) PROP with monoidal product induced by horizontal composition
\oplus
of Brauer diagrams and monoid of scalars
\mathsf{BD}(0,0)\cong\mathbb{N}
.

Example 3.13
.

By
3.11
, the symmetric groupoid
\Sigma
embeds canonically in
\mathsf{BD}
.

By definition,
\mathsf{BD}(m_{1},n_{1})\cong\mathsf{BD}(m_{2},n_{2})
for all
(m_{1},n_{1})
and
(m_{2},n_{2})
such that
m_{1}+n_{1}=m_{2}+n_{2}
.

In fact, the category
\mathsf{BD}
has a compact closed structure, for which all objects are self-dual. Given a Brauer diagram
f\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
:

•

its evaluation morphism
{\lfloor{f}\rfloor}=({\lfloor{\tau}\rfloor},\mathrm{k})\in\mathsf{BD}(n+m,0)
is induced by the canonical isomorphism

\{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}\amalg\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\ {\cong}\ \{t_{n},\dots,t_{1},s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}\amalg\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}

•

its coevaluation morphism
{\lceil{f}\rceil}=({\lceil{\tau}\rceil},\mathrm{k})\in\mathsf{BD}(0,n+m)
is induced by the canonical isomorphism

\{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}\amalg\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\ {\cong}\ \hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\amalg\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n},s_{m},\dots,s_{1}\}

•

its dual morphism
f^{*}=(\tau^{*},\mathrm{k})\in\mathsf{BD}(n,m)
is induced by the canonical isomorphism

\{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}\amalg\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\ {\cong}\ \{t_{n},\dots,t_{1}\}\amalg\{s_{m},\dots,s_{1}\}.

In particular, the morphisms
id_{1}\in\mathsf{BD}(1,1)
,
\cap\in\mathsf{BD}(0,2)
and
\cup\in\mathsf{BD}(2,0)
are the open Brauer diagrams induced by the unique pairing on the two-element set
\{s,t\}
.
And, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

id_{n}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}id_{1}\in\mathsf{BD}(n,n)
and

\cap_{n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\lceil{id_{n}}\rceil}\in\mathsf{BD}(0,2n)
, and
\cup_{n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\lfloor{id_{n}}\rfloor}\in\mathsf{BD}(2n,0)

satisfy the
n
-fold triangle identities (
Figure

4
).

(3.14)

(\cup_{n}\oplus id_{n})\circ(id_{n}\oplus\cap_{n})=id_{n}=(id_{n}\oplus\cup_{n})\circ(\cap_{n}\oplus id_{n}).

Remark 3.15
.

In this work, the direction of morphisms is depicted as top to bottom (source above target). Hence the designation of
\cup
and
\cap
may differ from other (Feynman diagram) conventions.

It is important to note that the subsets
\mathring{\mathsf{BD}}(m,n)\subset\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
of open Brauer diagrams do not describe a subcategory of
\mathsf{BD}
. Namely, the composition
\cup\circ\cap
defines the unit trace
\bigcirc=(\varnothing,1)\in\mathsf{BD}(0,0)
which is not open. And, for all
n\geq 0
,

\cup_{n}\circ\cap_{n}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}(\cup\circ\cap)=(\varnothing,n)\in\mathsf{BD}(0,0).

Figure 4.
The triangle identities in
\mathsf{BD}
with
n=3
.

Example 3.16
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring, and let
{R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
be its category of modules.
Let
\underline{\mathsf{BD}}_{R}
be the free
{R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
-category on
\mathsf{BD}
. So, for each pair
m,n
of natural numbers,
\underline{\mathsf{BD}}_{R}(m,n)
is the free
R
module (infinitely) generated by
\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
.
For each
\delta\in R
, let
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}=\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}^{R}
be the
{R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
-enriched
Brauer category

[
27
]
whose
objects are natural numbers
n\in\mathbb{N}
and,
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}(m,n)
is the free
R
-module (finitely) generated by the open Brauer diagrams in
\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
. Composition in
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
is defined by

\tau_{g}\tau_{f}=\delta^{\mathrm{k}_{gf}}\tau_{gf}\in\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}(k,n)\text{ for all generators }\tau_{f}\in\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}(k,m),\tau_{g}\in\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}(m,n).

If
R[t]
is the ring of polynomials in
R
, then the canonical isomorphisms
\underline{\mathsf{BD}}_{R}(m,n)\cong\mathsf{Br}_{t}^{R[t]}(m,n)
of
R
-modules induced by
(\tau,\mathrm{k})\mapsto t^{\mathrm{k}}\tau
extend to an isomorphism of
{R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
-enriched categories.

A
Brauer algebra
is an algebra of the form
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}(n,n)
, for some commutative ring
R
,
\delta\in R
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. They were introduced in
[
6
]
, where Brauer used them to study the representation theory of the orthogonal and symplectic groups.

In particular, Brauer proved that, if
R=\Bbbk
is a field of characteristic
0
and
V
is a
k
-dimensional vector space over
\Bbbk
, then representations of
\mathsf{Br}_{k}^{\Bbbk}(n,n)
in
V^{\otimes n}
are in one to one correspondence with degree
n
representations of the
k
-dimensional orthogonal group
\mathrm{O}(k)
on
V^{\otimes n}
. And, if
W
is a
2m
-dimensional vector space over
\Bbbk
, representations of
\mathsf{Br}_{(-m)}^{\Bbbk}(n,n)
in
W^{\otimes n}
are in one to one correspondence with degree
n
representations of the
m
-dimensional symplectic group
\mathrm{Sp}(m)
on
W^{\otimes n}
.

Definition 3.17
.

The subcategory
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}\subset\mathsf{BD}
of
downward Brauer diagrams
is the subcategory of open morphisms
(\tau^{\downarrow},0)\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
such that, for all
y\in\mathfrak{T}(f)
,

\tau^{\downarrow}(y)\in\mathfrak{S}(f)
. So,
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}(m,n)
is empty whenever
n>m
.

The category
\mathsf{BD}_{\uparrow}\subset\mathsf{BD}
of
upward Brauer diagrams
is the opposite category of
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
.

In particular,
\cap\not\in\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}(0,2)
,
\cup\not\in\mathsf{BD}_{\uparrow}(2,0)
. The intersection of
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
and
\mathsf{BD}_{\uparrow}
in
BD
is the symmetric groupoid
\Sigma
.

Example 3.18
.

For a commutative ring
R
, the
downwards Brauer category

\mathsf{Br}_{\downarrow}
is the free
{R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
-enriched category on
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
, and the
upwards Brauer category

\mathsf{Br}_{\uparrow}
is the free
{R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
-enriched category on
\mathsf{BD}_{\uparrow}
.

No closed components are formed by composition in
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
(or
\mathsf{BD}_{\uparrow}
). Hence
\mathsf{Br}_{\downarrow}
is a subcategory of
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
(and
\mathsf{Br}_{\uparrow}\subset\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
) for all
\delta\in R
.

In particular, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{Br}_{\downarrow}(n,n)=\mathsf{Br}_{\uparrow}(n,n)\cong R^{\Sigma_{n}}
is canonically a
\Sigma_{n}
-module. It follows from the results of
[
38
]
that symmetric strict monoidal functors
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}\to\mathsf{Vect}_{\scriptstyle{\Bbbk}}
describe representations of the
infinite orthogonal group

\mathrm{O}(\infty)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{O}(n)
.

Remark 3.19
.

By e.g.
[
27
]
, the category
\mathsf{BD}
is generated under horizontal and vertical composition by the morphisms
id_{1}
,
\cup
,
\cap
and the unique non-identity permutation
\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}\in\Sigma_{2}\subset\mathsf{BD}(2,2)
.

The category
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
is generated by
id_{1},\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}
, and
\cup
(and
\mathsf{BD}_{\uparrow}
is generated by
id_{1},\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}
, and
\cap
) under horizontal and vertical composition.

Example 3.20
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
denote the unit interval
[0,1]
, and let
\mathcal{M}\cong n_{o}(\mathcal{I})\amalg n_{c}(S^{1})
be a compact 1-manifold with canonical pairing
\tau^{\mathcal{M}}
on
\partial\mathcal{M}
as in
3.9
.
If
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
m+n=2n_{o}
, and
\phi\colon\{s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\}\amalg\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\to\partial\mathcal{M}
is any isomorphism, then

(\phi^{-1}\tau^{\mathcal{M}}\phi,n_{c})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
.

Conversely, given a morphism
f=(\tau,\mathrm{k})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
, there is a (unique up to isomorphism) pair
(\mathcal{M}_{f},\phi_{f})
of a compact 1-manifold
\mathcal{M}_{f}\cong\frac{m+n}{2}(\mathcal{I})\amalg\mathrm{k}(S^{1})
, and an isomorphism
\phi_{f}\colon\mathfrak{S}(f)\amalg\mathfrak{T}(f)\to\partial\mathcal{M}
such that
\phi^{-1}_{f}\tau^{\mathcal{M}_{f}}\phi_{f}=\tau
.

Following
3.20
, define
\partial f\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathfrak{S}(f)\amalg\mathfrak{T}(f)
to be the
boundary of
f=(\tau,\mathrm{k})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
. So
\partial f
consists of
m+n
points.

The set
\pi_{0}(f)

of components of
f
is the set of components of a compact manifold
\mathcal{M}_{f}
as in
3.20
.
So,
|\pi_{0}(f)|=\frac{(m+n)}{2}+\mathrm{k}
, and a morphism
f=(\tau,\mathrm{k})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
may also be written as a diagram of cospans of finite sets (where the arrow
\partial f\to\pi_{0}(f)
is the canonical map):

(3.21)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S_{i}\ \mapsto\tau(S_{i})}
\textstyle{\partial f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{T}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{j}\ \mapsto\tau(t_{j})}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(f).}

Remark 3.22
.

By (
3.21
), for composable morphisms
f\in\mathsf{BD}(k,m)
and
g\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
, we may consider the pushout diagram:

(3.23)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{T}(f)=\mathfrak{S}(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{g}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{T}(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{g}}
\textstyle{\partial f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial g\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P(gf)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi^{P}(gf).}

In general,
P(gf)\not\cong\partial(gf)=\mathfrak{S}(f)\amalg\mathfrak{T}(g)
and hence composition of morphisms in
\mathsf{BD}
is not described by compositions (pushouts) of cospans as in (
3.23
). (So
\mathsf{BD}
is not a
cospan category
.)

For example, in
the pushout (
3.23
) for the composition
\cup\circ\cap=\bigcirc\in\mathsf{BD}(0,0)
,
P(\bigcirc)
is a two element set. But
\bigcirc\in\mathsf{BD}(0,0)
so
\partial\bigcirc=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
. (This is closely related to the
problem of loops
discussed in detail in
[
34
, Section 6]
. See also
7.3
.)

Instead, to represent general composition of all Brauer diagrams in
\mathsf{BD}

in terms of pushouts of cospans, one can replace a diagram of the form (
3.21
) with a diagram of manifolds:

(3.24)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi\circ\tau}
\textstyle{\partial\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
inc
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\partial\mathcal{M}}
inc
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathfrak{T}(f),}
\scriptstyle{\phi\circ\tau}

where,
(\mathcal{M},\phi)
describes
f\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
as in
3.20
, and the isomorphism
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{0}(f)
is fixed by
\phi
on open components of
\mathcal{M}
.

Then, if
(\mathcal{M}_{f},\phi_{f})
represents
f=(\tau_{f},\mathrm{k}_{f})\in\mathsf{BD}(k,m)
, and

(\mathcal{M}_{g},\phi_{g})
represents
g=(\tau_{g},\mathrm{k}_{g})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
, the composition
gf\in\mathsf{BD}(k,n)
is obtained from the pushout of cospans of manifolds:

(3.25)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{S}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\text{inc }\circ\phi_{f}\circ\tau_{f}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{T}(f)=\mathfrak{S}(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\text{inc }\circ\phi_{f}\circ\tau_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\text{inc }\circ\phi_{g}\circ\tau_{g}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{T}(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\text{inc }\circ\phi_{g}\circ\tau_{g}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{f}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}_{f}\amalg_{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathfrak{T}(f)}}\mathcal{M}_{g}.}

3.3.
Coloured Brauer diagrams

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a set, and let
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathfrak{C}^{n}
denote the set of finite ordered sets
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})
of elements of
\mathfrak{C}
. So
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
underlies the free associative monoid on
\mathfrak{C}
. For
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})
and
\bm{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
is
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
, their (concatenation) product
\bm{c}\bm{d}=\bm{c}\oplus\bm{d}
is given by

\bm{c}\bm{d}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(c_{1},\dots,c_{m},d_{1},\dots,d_{n}).

The empty list
\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(-)
is the unit for
\oplus
.

The symmetric groupoid
\Sigma
acts on
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
from the right by
\sigma\colon\bm{c}\mapsto(\bm{c}\sigma)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(c_{\sigma 1},\dots,c_{\sigma m})
, for all
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{m}
.

Definition 3.26
.

A pair
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
of a set
\mathfrak{C}
together with an involution
\omega\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}

is called an
(involutive) palette
. Elements
c\in\mathfrak{C}
are called
colours in
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
. The set of orbits of
\omega
in
\mathfrak{C}
is denoted by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}
.

Objects of the category
\mathsf{Pal}
are palettes
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
, and morphisms
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)\to(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
are given by morphisms
\lambda\in\mathsf{Set}(\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{C}^{\prime})
that commute with the involution such that
\lambda\circ\omega=\omega^{\prime}\circ\lambda
.

For any palette
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
, the induced involutions
\bm{c}\mapsto{\overleftarrow{\bm{c}}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(c_{m},\dots,c_{1})
and
\bm{c}\mapsto\omega\bm{c}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(\omega c_{1},\dots,\omega c_{m})
on
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
commute. Hence their composite
\bm{\overleftarrow{\omega}}
also defines an involution on
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
:

\bm{\overleftarrow{\omega}}(\bm{c})\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\overleftarrow{\omega\bm{c}}}=\omega({\overleftarrow{\bm{c}}})=(\omega c_{m},\dots,\omega c_{1})\ \text{for all }\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}.

Now let
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
be any palette, and let
(X,\tau)
be the palette described by a pairing
\tau
on a finite set
X
.

Definition 3.27
.

A
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-colouring of
\tau
is morphism
\lambda\colon(X,\tau)\to(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
in
\mathsf{Pal}
.

A
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-colouring
\lambda
of a Brauer diagram
f=(\tau,\mathrm{k})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
is given by a pair
\lambda=({{\lambda}_{\partial}},{\widetilde{\lambda}})
where
{{\lambda}_{\partial}}
is a colouring of
\tau
and
{\widetilde{\lambda}}
is a map
\pi_{0}(f)\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}
such that the following diagram of sets commutes:

(3.28)

\textstyle{\partial f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\partial}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\partial f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\partial}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}(f)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\lambda}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}.}

The
type of the colouring
\lambda

is the pair
(\bm{c},\bm{d})\in(\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C})^{2}
– where
\bm{c}
is called the
input type
, and
\bm{d}
is called the
output type
, of
(f,\lambda)
– defined by:

(3.29)

\bm{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})=\lambda_{\partial}(\mathfrak{T}(f)),\text{ and }\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})=\omega\circ\lambda_{\partial}(\mathfrak{S}(f)).

Remark 3.30
.

The application of
\omega
in the definition of the input type
\bm{c}=\omega\circ{{\lambda}_{\partial}}(\mathfrak{S}(f))
is necessary to define categorical composition of coloured Brauer diagrams in
3.31
.

Given
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})
and
\bm{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
in
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
, objects of the set
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c},\bm{d})
of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured Brauer diagrams from
\bm{c}
to
\bm{d}
are pairs
(f,\lambda)
where
f=(\tau,\mathrm{k})
is a morphism in
\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
, and
\lambda
is a colouring of
f
of type
(\bm{c},\bm{d})
.

Horizontal composition
\oplus
of coloured Brauer diagrams
(f,\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1},\bm{d}_{1})
and
(g,\gamma)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{2},\bm{d}_{2})
is given by juxtaposition and concatenation:

(f,\lambda)\oplus(f,\gamma)=(f\oplus g,\lambda\amalg\gamma)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1}\bm{c}_{2},\bm{d}_{1}\bm{d}_{2}).

Let
(f,\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{b},\bm{c})
and
(g,\gamma)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c},\bm{d})
with
f=(\tau_{f},\mathrm{k}_{f})\in\mathsf{BD}(k,m)
and
g=(\tau_{g}\mathrm{k}_{g})\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
be such that
gf=(\tau_{gf},\mathrm{k}_{gf})\in\mathsf{BD}(k,n)
. By definition,
\gamma_{\partial}(y)=\omega\lambda_{\partial}(y)
for each
y\in\mathfrak{T}(f)=\mathfrak{S}(f)
.
Hence,
for each
x\in\mathfrak{S}(f)
,
\lambda_{\partial}(\tau_{gf})(x)=\omega\lambda_{\partial}(x)
. And likewise, for
z\in\mathfrak{T}(g)
,
\gamma_{\partial}(\tau_{gf})(z)=\omega\gamma_{\partial}(z)
. Therefore, there is a well-defined colouring
(\gamma\lambda)_{\partial}
of
\tau_{gf}
that restricts to
\lambda_{\partial}
on
\mathfrak{S}(f)
and to
\gamma_{\partial}
on
\mathfrak{T}(g)
.

Moreover, for each closed component
[y]=(y,y_{1}=\tau_{f}(y),y_{2}=\tau_{g}(y_{1}),\dots)\subset\mathfrak{T}(f)
created by the composition
g\circ f
, there is a well-defined element
\tilde{c}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}
given by the class of
{\widetilde{\lambda}}(y_{i})
(
y_{i}\in[y]
) in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}
. Therefore, the composition
g\circ f

induces a well-defined map
\widetilde{\gamma\lambda}\colon\pi_{0}(gf)\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}
(see
Figure

5
).

It follows that
\left((\gamma\lambda)_{\partial},\widetilde{\gamma\lambda}\right)
describes a colouring
\gamma\lambda
on
\tau_{gf}
.

\omega c
d
\omega d
\omega d

d
\omega d
\omega d

\omega c

Composing coloured pairings.

Definition 3.31
.

Objects of the category
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured Brauer diagrams
are elements of
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
. Morphisms in
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c},\bm{d})
are
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured Brauer diagrams of type
(\bm{c},\bm{d})
, with composition of morphisms
(f,\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{b},\bm{c})
and
(g,\gamma)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c},\bm{d})
is given by
(gf,\gamma\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{b},\bm{d})
.

The category
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
is a
\mathfrak{C}
-coloured PROP (see
Section

3.1
), with monoidal structure
\oplus
and unit
O\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}},\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}})
, induced by concatenation of object lists and disjoint union of coloured Brauer diagrams. It has a compact closed structure given by
\bm{c}^{*}={\overleftarrow{\omega\bm{c}}}
for all
\bm{c}
.

If
f\in\mathsf{BD}(m,n)
is such that
(f,\lambda)
is a morphism in
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c},\bm{d})
then the

•

evaluation morphism

{\lfloor{(f,\lambda)}\rfloor}
is given by
({\lfloor{f}\rfloor},\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(({\overleftarrow{\omega\bm{d}}})\oplus\bm{c},\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}})
;

•

coevaluation morphism

{\lceil{(f,\lambda)}\rceil}
is given by
({\lceil{f}\rceil},\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}},\bm{d}\oplus({\overleftarrow{\omega\bm{c}}}))
;

•

dual morphism

(f,\lambda)^{*}
is given by
(f^{*},\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(({\overleftarrow{\omega\bm{d}}}),({\overleftarrow{\omega\bm{c}}}))
.

\mathrm{tr}(id_{\bm{c}})
.

Example 3.32
.

(See also
4.10
.)
Let
\{\wideparen{+,-}\}
be the palette given by the unique non-trivial involution
+\leftrightarrow-
on the two element set
\{+,-\}
.
Then
\mathsf{BD}^{\scriptstyle{{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}}
is the category of
oriented Brauer diagrams
. This is the free compact closed category with duals on a single object, or, equivalently, the quotient of the
oriented tangle category

[
44
]
, obtained by identifying over and under-crossings.
Morphisms in
\mathsf{BD}^{\scriptstyle{{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}}
are represented, as in
Figure

7
, by diagrams of oriented intervals and unoriented circles.

Let
\Bbbk

3.16
, the Brauer categories
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
,
\delta\in\Bbbk
. For
\delta\in\Bbbk
, the
walled Brauer category

W\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}\subset\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
– studied in the representation theory of general linear groups
[
46
,
38
]
– is the subcategory of
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
whose objects are given by pairs
(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, and
W\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}((m_{1},n_{1}),(m_{2},n_{2}))\subset\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}(m_{1}+n_{1},m_{2}+n_{2}))
is subspace spanned by pairings
\tau
that correspond to bijections

\{s^{+}_{1},\dots,s^{+}_{m_{1}}\}\amalg\{t^{-}_{1},\dots,t^{-}_{n_{2}}\}\xrightarrow{\cong}\{s^{-}_{1},\dots,s^{-}_{n_{1}}\}\amalg\{t^{+}_{1},\dots,t^{+}_{m_{1}}\}.

Since every element of
\small{\mathsf{list}}\{+,-\}
is isomorphic – via a canonical shuffle permutation – to a unique element of the form
(+,\dots,+,-,\dots,-)
, there is a faithful functor
\mathsf{BD}^{\scriptstyle{{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}}\to W\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
that takes open morphisms in
\mathsf{BD}^{\scriptstyle{{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}}
to generating diagrams in
W\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}
.
