Figure 7.
Composing directed Brauer diagrams
f\colon(-,-,+)\to(-,+,-,-,+,-,+)
and
g\colon(-,+,-,-,+,-,+)\to(-,+,+,-,-)
. Up to a shuffle permutation, this is equivalent to a composition of walled Brauer diagrams, where horizontal arrows go from left to right.

More generally, if
\mathfrak{D}
is a set, and
\mathfrak{C}=\mathfrak{D}\times\{\wideparen{+,-}\}
with involution
\omega:(c,+)\mapsto(c,-)
, then
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
is the free compact closed category with objects generated by
\mathfrak{D}
.

3.4.
Functors from categories of Brauer diagrams

A symmetric monoidal functor
(\mathcal{A},\eta_{\mathcal{A}},\alpha)\colon\mathsf{BD}\to\mathcal{V}
induces interesting algebraic structure on its image in
\mathcal{V}
.

By definition,
(\mathcal{A}(n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
describes an
\mathbb{N}
-graded monoid in
\mathcal{V}
with product
\boxtimes
and unit induced by the structure morphisms

\boxtimes_{m,n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\alpha_{(m,n)}\colon\mathcal{A}(m)\otimes\mathcal{A}(n)\to\mathcal{A}(m+n),\text{ and }\eta_{\mathcal{A}}\colon I\to\mathcal{A}(0).

The product
\boxtimes
is associative up to associators in
\mathcal{V}
.

Moreover, for all
n\geq 2
, and all
1\leq i<j<n
, there is a canonical
contraction
morphism

\zeta^{i\ddagger j}_{n}\colon\mathcal{A}(n)\to\mathcal{A}(n-2)
induced by the open Brauer diagram
(\cup\oplus id_{n-2})\circ\rho^{i,j}_{n}\in\mathsf{BD}(n,n-2)
(see
Figure

8
(a))), where
\rho^{i,j}_{n}\in\Sigma_{n}
is the shuffle permutation
on
\mathbf{n}=\{1,\dots,n\}

given by
i\mapsto 1,j\mapsto 2
, whilst leaving the relative order of the remaining elements unchanged.

The contraction commutes in the sense that, given
1\leq k<m\leq n
such that
i,j,k,m
are all distinct,

(3.33)

\zeta^{k^{\prime}\ddagger m^{\prime}}_{n-2}\circ\zeta^{i\ddagger j}_{n}=\zeta^{i^{\prime}\ddagger j^{\prime}}_{n-2}\circ\zeta^{k\ddagger m}_{n}\colon\mathcal{A}(n)\to\mathcal{A}(n-4).

Here
1\leq k^{\prime}<m^{\prime}\leq n-2
and
1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq n-2
are the obvious adjusted indices
of
k,m
and
i,j
in
\{1,\dots,n-2\}
.
(See
Figure

8
(b).)

(a)
\zeta^{2\ddagger 5}_{5}
(b)
\zeta^{1\ddagger 3}_{4}\circ\zeta^{1\ddagger 4}_{6}=\zeta^{1\ddagger 3}_{4}\circ\zeta^{2\ddagger 5}_{6}

Figure 8.
Brauer diagrams for contraction

Together, the contraction
\zeta
and monoidal product
\boxtimes
induce a ‘multiplication’ operation
\diamond
(see
5.8
), by

(3.34)

\diamond_{m,n}^{i\ddagger j}=\zeta^{i\ddagger m+j}_{m+n}\circ\boxtimes_{m,n}\colon\mathcal{A}(m)\otimes\mathcal{A}(n)\to\mathcal{A}(m+n-2),

for all
m,n\geq 1
and all
1\leq i\leq m
,
1\leq j\leq n
.

Let
\rho_{n\leadsto i}\in\Sigma_{n}
be the permutation
n\mapsto i
whilst leaving the relative order of the remaining elements of
\mathbf{n}
unchanged. By the triangle identities (
3.14
),

(\cup\oplus\rho_{n\leadsto i})\circ\rho_{n+2}^{i,n+1}\circ(id_{n}\oplus\cap)=id_{n}\text{ in }\mathsf{BD}.

Hence,
the image of the cap morphism
\cap\in\mathsf{BD}(0,2)
induces a two-sided unit
\epsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\colon I\mapsto\mathcal{A}(2)
for the multiplication
\diamond
on
\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 3.35
.

A symmetric functor
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}\to(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
from the category of downward Brauer diagrams induces contraction and multiplication operations on its image in
\mathcal{V}
. However, since
\cap
is not a morphism in
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
, there is no unit for this multiplication (see also
[
34
,
39
]
).

The coloured case follows in the same way: If
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
is a palette, then a functor
(\mathcal{A},\alpha,\eta_{\mathcal{A}})\colon\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}\to(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
equips the
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
-indexed object
(\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}}
with an, up to associators in
\mathcal{V}
, associative unital graded monoidal product via
\alpha
. The induced product
\boxtimes
has unit
I\mapsto\mathcal{A}(\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}})
.

A contraction
\zeta
and multiplication
\boxtimes
on
(\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}}
are obtained exactly as in the monochrome case. And, once again,
\cap_{c}\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}},(c,\omega c))
induces a distinguished morphism
\mathcal{A}(\cap_{c})\colon I\to\mathcal{A}(c,\omega c)
that acts as a two-sided unit for
\diamond
for all
c\in\mathfrak{C}
.

4.
Circuit algebras

Circuit algebras – defined in
Section

4.2
– are algebraic structures described by a small family
(A_{c})_{c}
of algebraic objects (usually vector spaces). Their operations are governed by wiring diagrams, which are, essentially, non-planar versions of Jones’s planar diagrams
[
17
]
.

Wiring diagrams are commonly described by partitioning boundaries of 1-manifolds (e.g.
[
9
,
10
]
). However, they
admit a straightforward description in terms of
Brauer diagrams, and this is the approach of this paper.

4.1.
Operads preliminaries

This section summarises the basic theory of (coloured) operads.
See
[
28
]
and
[
4
]
for more details and precise definitions of coloured operads (called multicategories in
[
28
]
) and their algebras.

As in
Section

3.3
, let
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{D}
denote the set of finite lists
\bm{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
on a set
\mathfrak{D}
. Recall that a (symmetric)
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured operad
\mathcal{O}
(in the category of sets) is given by a
(\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{D}\times\mathfrak{D})
-graded set
(\mathcal{O}(c_{1},\dots,c_{m};d))_{(c_{1},\dots,c_{m};d)}
, and
a family of
composition morphisms

\gamma\colon\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)\times\left(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{O}(\bm{b}_{i};c_{i})\right)\to\mathcal{O}(\bm{b}_{1}\dots\bm{b}_{m};d)

defined for all
d\in\mathfrak{D}
,
\bm{c}=(c_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
and all
\bm{b}_{i}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{D}
(
1\leq i\leq m
).

For
\phi\in\mathcal{O}(c_{1},\dots,c_{m};d)
,
d
is called the
output
of
\phi
and each
c_{i}
is an
input
of
\phi
. The symmetric groupoid
\Sigma
acts on
\mathcal{O}
by permuting the inputs: each
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
induces isomorphisms

\mathcal{O}(c_{1},\dots,c_{n};d)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{O}(c_{\sigma 1},\dots,c_{\sigma n};d).

The composition
\gamma
is required to be associative and equivariant with respect to the
\Sigma
-action on
\mathcal{O}
.

For all
d\in D
, there is an element
\nu_{d}\in\mathcal{O}(d;d)
such that
for all
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{D}
, the morphisms given by the composites

\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)\xrightarrow{\cong}I\times\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)\xrightarrow{(\nu_{d},id)}\mathcal{O}(d;d)\times\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)\xrightarrow{\gamma}\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d),

and

\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)\times I\\
\xrightarrow{(id,\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\nu_{c_{i}})}\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)\times\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{O}(c_{i};c_{i})\right)\xrightarrow{\gamma}\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d),

are the identity on
\mathcal{O}(\bm{c};d)
.

Example 4.1
.

In particular, the restriction of a
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured operad
\mathcal{O}
to the sets
\mathcal{O}(c;d)
( for
c,d\in\mathfrak{D}
) describes a small category. Conversely, a small category
\mathsf{C}
with object set
\mathsf{C}_{0}
describes a
\mathsf{C}_{0}
-coloured operad
C
such that
C(x;y)=\mathsf{C}(x,y)
for all
x,y\in\mathsf{C}
and
C(x_{1},\dots,x_{k};y)=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
for
k\neq 1
.

The following example is somewhat more interesting:

Example 4.2
.

Underlying any (small) permutative category
(\mathsf{C},\oplus,0)
, with object set
\mathsf{C}_{0}
, is a
\mathsf{C}_{0}
-coloured (symmetric) operad
\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}
,
defined by

\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};y)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{C}(x_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus x_{n},y),

and
with operadic composition
\gamma
in
\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}
induced by composition in
\mathsf{C}
:

\gamma\colon\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};y)\times\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}(w_{i,1},\dots,w_{i,m_{i}};x_{i})\right)\to\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}(w_{1,1},\dots,w_{i,m_{i}},\dots,w_{n,m_{n}};y),

\left(\overline{g},(\overline{f}_{i})_{i}\right)\mapsto\overline{\left(g\circ(f_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus f_{n})\right)}

for all
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};y)
described by
g\in\mathsf{C}(x_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus x_{n},y)
, and
\overline{f}_{i}\in\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}(w_{i,1},\dots,w_{i,m_{i}};x_{i})
described by
f_{i}\in\mathsf{C}(w_{i,1}\oplus\dots\oplus w_{i,m_{i}},x_{i})
(
1\leq i\leq n
).

(In fact, any monoidal category has an underlying operad
[
28
, Example 2.1.3]
, but that is not needed in this work.)

Objects of the category
\mathsf{Op}
are (coloured) operads.
For
i=1,2
, let
(\mathcal{O}^{i},\gamma^{i},\nu^{i})
be a
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
-coloured operad. A morphism
\mathcal{A}\colon(\mathcal{O}^{1},\gamma^{1},\nu^{1})\to(\mathcal{O}^{2},\gamma^{2},\nu^{2})
is given by
a map of sets
\mathcal{A}_{0}\colon\mathfrak{D}_{1}\to\mathfrak{D}_{2}
, and a
(\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{D}_{1}\times\mathfrak{D}_{1})
-indexed family of maps

\mathcal{A}_{(c_{1},\dots,c_{k};d)}\colon\mathcal{O}^{1}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k};d)\to\mathcal{O}^{2}(\mathcal{A}_{0}(c_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{A}_{0}(c_{k});\mathcal{A}_{0}(d))

that respect units and composition, and are equivariant with respect to the action of
\Sigma
.

If
\mathcal{A}_{0}=id_{\mathfrak{D}}
, with
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}_{1}=\mathfrak{D}_{2}
, then
\mathcal{A}\colon\mathcal{O}^{1}\to\mathcal{O}^{2}
is called
colour-preserving
. The category of
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured operads and colour-preserving morphisms is denoted by
\mathsf{Op}^{\mathfrak{D}}
.

Example 4.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
be a symmetric monoidal category. Given a set
\mathfrak{D}
and a
\mathfrak{D}
-indexed object
A=(A(c))_{c\in\mathfrak{D}}
in
\mathcal{V}
, the
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured
endomorphism operad
End^{A}
is given by

End^{A}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k};d)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathcal{V}\left(A(c_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes A(c_{k}),A(d)\right),

together with the obvious composition and units induced by composition and identities in
\mathcal{V}
.

Let
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
be a symmetric monoidal category and
\mathcal{O}
a
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured operad.

Definition 4.4
.

A
\mathcal{V}
-algebra for
\mathcal{O}
is a
\mathfrak{D}
-indexed object
(A(c))_{c}
in
\mathcal{V}
, together with a morphism
\mathcal{A}\colon\mathcal{O}\to End^{A}
of
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured operads.

The category
\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{O})
of
\mathcal{V}
-algebras for
\mathcal{O}
is the subcategory of the slice category
\mathcal{O}/\mathsf{Op}
whose objects are of the form
(A,\mathcal{A})
with
A=(A(c))_{c}
a
\mathfrak{D}
-graded object in
\mathcal{V}
. A morphism
g\colon(A,\mathcal{A})\to(B,\mathcal{B})
in
\mathcal{O}/\mathsf{Op}
is a morphism in
\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{O})
if and only if there are morphisms
g_{c}\in\mathcal{V}(A(c),B(c))
(for all
c\in\mathfrak{D}
), such that, for all
c_{1},\dots,c_{k},d\in\mathfrak{D}
and all
\phi\in End^{A}(c_{1},\dots,c_{k};d)
, the following diagram commutes in
\mathcal{V}
:

\textstyle{A(c_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes A(c_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{g_{c_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes g_{c_{k}}}
\textstyle{B(c_{1})\otimes\dots\otimes B(c_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g(\phi)}
\textstyle{A(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{d}}
\textstyle{B(d).}

Remark 4.5
.

Although

4.4
relies on the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathcal{V}
, it is concerned with operads in the category of sets and does not involve operads enriched in a (closed) symmetric monoidal category.

If
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}
is the operad underlying a small permutative category
\mathsf{C}
, then a
\mathcal{V}
-algebra for
\mathcal{O}
describes a functor
\mathsf{C}\to\mathcal{V}
. It follows from e.g.
[
28
, Chapters 2-3,]
(and particularly Theorem 3.3.4(b) there) that:

Lemma 4.6
.

The category
\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}})
of
\mathcal{V}
-algebras for the operad
\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{C}}
underlying a small permutative category
(\mathsf{C},\oplus,0)
is
canonically isomorphic to the category of symmetric lax monoidal functors
\mathcal{A}\colon(\mathsf{C},\oplus,0)\to(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
.

4.2.
Wiring diagrams and circuit algebras

Circuit algebras are algebras over an operad of wiring diagrams. Wiring diagrams are often defined (for example in
[
9
,
10
]
) as equivalence classes of immersions of compact 1-manifolds in punctured 2-discs (see
4.8
).

This work takes a different approach.

Definition 4.7
.

For a given palette
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
, the
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
-coloured operad of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-wiring diagrams
is the operad
\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathcal{O}^{{\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}}}
underlying
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
(
4.2
).

For each
(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}^{2}\mathfrak{C}
, elements of the set

\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})

are called
wiring diagrams of type
(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})
.

When
\mathfrak{C}
is the singleton set, the
\mathbb{N}
-coloured operad
\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathcal{O}^{\mathsf{BD}}
is denoted by
\mathrm{WD}
, and called the
operad of (monochrome) wiring diagrams
.

Figure 9.
Composition in
\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
. (See also
Figure

10
.)

Remark 4.8
.

Wiring diagrams are often defined (for example in
[
9
,
10
]
) as equivalence classes of immersions of compact 1-manifolds in punctured 2-discs. According to this representation, wiring diagrams form an operad with composition described by inserting discs into punctures as in
Figure

10
. From this point of view, the composition in
Figure

9
is represented as in
Figure

10
.

On this interpretation, operads of coloured wiring diagrams may be defined by colouring 1-manifolds according to
3.20
and
3.27
.

This visualisation of wiring diagrams illustrates the relationship between wiring diagrams (and hence circuit algebras) and planar diagrams and algebras
[
18
]
. It also clearly exhibits the operad of monochrome wiring diagrams as a suboperad of the operad of wiring diagrams defined in
[
41
]
.

Moreover, the disc representation of wiring diagrams is highly suggestive of the graphical constructions that will follow in Sections
6
-
10
.

However, the definition in terms of Brauer diagrams is more concise and, furthermore, reveals the deep connections between circuit algebras, representations of classical groups and, via Theorems
9.9
and
10.4
, the combinatorics of modular operads.

Figure 10.
Disc representation of the composition in
Figure

9
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a symmetric monoidal category. The definition of circuit algebras in
[
1
,
9
]
is generalised by:

Definition 4.9
.

A
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebra
is a
\mathcal{V}
-valued algebra for the operad
\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured wiring diagrams.
The full subcategory of
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebras in
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)})
is denoted by
{\mathsf{CA}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}_{\mathcal{V}}
. When
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Set}
,
{\mathsf{CA}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}_{\mathcal{V}}
is denoted simply by
{\mathsf{CA}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
.

Example 4.10
.

As in
3.32
, let
\mathfrak{Di}
be the palette described by the unique non-trivial involution on the two element set
\{+,-\}
. For any set
\mathfrak{D}
, there is a palette
\mathfrak{D}\times\mathfrak{Di}
given by the involution
(c,+)\leftrightarrow(c,-)
, for all
c\in\mathfrak{D}
.

If
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)=\mathfrak{D}\times\mathfrak{Di}
for some set
\mathfrak{D}
, then
\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
is an operad of
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured oriented wiring diagrams
, and
\mathfrak{D}\times\mathfrak{Di}
-coloured circuit algebras are
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured oriented circuit algebras
.

These are described in detail in
[
9
,
10
]
. In
[
9
]
, it is proved that
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured oriented
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebras in
\mathcal{V}
are equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}
-coloured wheeled PROPs in
\mathcal{V}
. Hence
\mathfrak{Di}
-coloured circuit algebras are (monochrome) wheeled PROPs. These have been described in
[
32
,
33
]
and have applications in geometry and deformation theory.

It follows immediately from
4.6
and
4.7
that:

Theorem 4.11
.

The category
{\mathsf{CA}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}_{\mathcal{V}}
of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebras is isomorphic to the category of symmetric monoidal functors
\mathcal{A}\colon\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}\to\mathcal{V}
.

Example 4.12
.

In particular, if
R
is a commutative ring, and
\mathcal{V}={R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
, then a monochrome
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebra
\mathcal{A}
describes a sequence of
R
-modules, each equipped with a pairing
\mathcal{A}(n)\otimes\mathcal{A}(n)\to R
induced by
\mathcal{A}(\cup_{n})
.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}(0)=R
and
\mathcal{A}(m)\otimes\mathcal{A}(n)\cong\mathcal{A}(m+n)
, then
\mathcal{A}
corresponds to a symmetric strong monoidal functor
\mathsf{BD}\to{R}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Mod}
. In this case, the pairing on
\mathcal{A}(n)
is non-degenerate for each
n
and
\mathcal{A}(n)
describes a Brauer algebra
\mathsf{Br}_{\delta}(n,n)
with
\delta=\mathcal{A}(\bigcirc)\in R
.

The notation
\mathcal{A}
will be used to denote both a circuit algebra
(A,\mathcal{A})
and the corresponding functor
\mathcal{A}\colon\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}\to\mathcal{V}
.

The assignment
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)\mapsto{\mathsf{CA}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}_{\mathcal{V}}
defines a
\mathsf{Cat}
-valued presheaf
ca_{\mathcal{V}}
on the palette category
{\mathsf{Pal}}
: a morphism
\phi\colon(\mathfrak{C},\omega)\to(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{Pal}
induces a strict symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}\to\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})}
, and hence any
(\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
coloured circuit algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
pulls back to a
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured circuit algebra
\phi^{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

For any symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{V}
, let
{\mathsf{CA}}_{\mathcal{V}}
be the category of
all

\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebras whose objects are pairs
((\mathfrak{C},\omega),\mathcal{A})
of a palette
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
and a
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebra
\mathcal{A}
, and whose morphisms
((\mathfrak{C},\omega),\mathcal{A})\to((\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}),\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
are pairs
(\phi,\alpha)
where
\phi\colon\mathfrak{C}\to\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
satisfies
\phi\omega=\omega^{\prime}\phi
and
\alpha\colon\phi^{*}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{A}
is a morphism of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured circuit algebras.

In other words
{\mathsf{CA}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is the Grothendieck construction of the functor
ca_{A}\colon\mathsf{Pal}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Cat}
. When
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)=(\mathsf{Set},\times,*)
,
{\mathsf{CA}}_{\mathcal{V}}
is denoted by
{\mathsf{CA}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\mathsf{CA}}_{\mathsf{Set}}
.

By
4.11
, a
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebra
consists of
a collection
(\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}}
of objects and, for each
(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}^{2}\mathfrak{C}\times\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
, a set of
\mathcal{V}
-morphisms
\mathcal{A}{(\overline{f},\lambda)}\colon\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}_{i})\to\mathcal{A}(\bm{d})
indexed by Brauer diagrams
(f,\lambda)\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\bm{c}_{k},\bm{d})
.

These satisfy:

•

for all
\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
,
\mathcal{A}({\overline{id}_{\bm{c}}})=id_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{c})}\in\mathcal{V}(\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}),\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}))
;

•

the morphisms
\mathcal{A}{(\overline{f},\lambda)}
are equivariant with respect to the
\Sigma
-action on
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
and on
\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
;

•

given wiring diagrams
(\overline{f},\lambda)\in\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})
, and, for all
1\leq i\leq k
,
(\overline{f}^{i},\lambda^{i})\in\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{b}_{i,1},\dots,\bm{b}_{i,k_{i}};\bm{c}_{i})
, the diagram

(4.13)

\textstyle{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\mathcal{A}(\bm{b}_{i,j})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}{\left(\gamma\left((\overline{f},\lambda),(\overline{f}^{i},\lambda^{i})_{i}\right)\right)}\hskip 16.38895pt{}}
\scriptstyle{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}{(\overline{f}^{i},\lambda^{i})}}
\textstyle{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}{(\overline{f},\lambda)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}(\bm{d})}

commutes in
\mathcal{V}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured
\mathcal{V}
-circuit algebra.

Proposition 4.14
.

The collection
(\mathcal{A}({\bm{c}}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}}
describes a
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
-graded monoid
(\mathcal{A},\boxtimes,\mathcal{A}(\varnothing_{\mathfrak{C}}))
in
\mathcal{V}
, equipped with
contraction maps
\zeta_{\bm{c}}^{i\ddagger j}\colon\mathcal{A}(\bm{c})\to\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}_{\widehat{i,j}})
for all
1\leq i<j\leq m
and all
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
such that
c_{i}=\omega c_{j}
.
These satisfy:

(c1)

the graded monoidal product
\boxtimes
on
(\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}}
is associative up to associators in
\mathcal{V}
;

(c2)

contractions commute:

\zeta^{i^{\prime}\ddagger j^{\prime}}_{\bm{c}_{\widehat{k,m}}}\circ\zeta^{k\ddagger m}_{\bm{c}}=\zeta^{k^{\prime}\ddagger m^{\prime}}_{\bm{c}_{\widehat{i,j}}}\circ\zeta^{i\ddagger j}_{\bm{c}}\colon\mathcal{A}({\bm{c}})\to\mathcal{A}({\bm{c}_{\widehat{i,j,k,m}}})\ \text{ wherever defined};

(c3)

contraction commutes with monoidal product:

\zeta^{i\ddagger j}_{\bm{c}\bm{d}}\ \circ\ \boxtimes_{\bm{c},\bm{d}}=\boxtimes_{\bm{c}_{\widehat{i,j}}\oplus\bm{d}}\ \circ\ (\zeta^{i\ddagger j}_{\bm{c}}\otimes id_{\mathcal{A}(\bm{d})})\colon\mathcal{A}(\bm{c})\otimes\mathcal{A}(\bm{d})\to\mathcal{A}(\bm{c}_{\widehat{i,j}}\bm{d})

for all
\bm{d}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
and
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
with
c_{i}=\omega c_{j}
,
1\leq i<j\leq m
.

Moreover, for each
c\in\mathfrak{C}
, there is a distinguished morphism
\epsilon_{c}\in\mathcal{V}(I,\mathcal{A}{(c,\omega c)})
such that

(e1)

\epsilon_{c}
satisfies
\zeta_{\bm{c}\oplus(c,\omega c)}^{i\ddagger 2}(id_{\bm{c}}\boxtimes\epsilon_{c})=id_{\bm{c}}
for all
\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
and all
i
such that
c_{i}=c
.

Proof.

By
4.11
,
\mathcal{A}
describes a symmetric monoidal functor
(\mathcal{A},\alpha,\eta_{\alpha})\colon\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}\to\mathcal{V}
and so the proposition follows from
Section

3.4
. In particular, the monoidal product
\boxtimes
on
(\mathcal{A}({\bm{c}}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}}
is induced by
\alpha
and the maps
\overline{id_{\bm{c}\bm{d}}}\in\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c},\bm{d};\bm{c}\bm{d})
corresponding to the identity morphisms
id_{\bm{c}\bm{d}}\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}\bm{d},\bm{c}\bm{d})
.
∎

Let
\mathrm{WD}_{\downarrow}\subset\mathrm{WD}
be the suboperad of (monochrome)
downward wiring diagrams
. This is the operad underlying the category
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
of downward Brauer diagrams.

Corollary 4.15
.

A
\mathcal{V}
-algebra for
\mathrm{WD}_{\downarrow}
is a graded monoid
(\mathcal{A}(n)_{n},\boxtimes,\mathcal{A}(0))
in
\mathcal{V}
equipped with a contraction operation
\zeta
, such that the monoid multiplication
\boxtimes
and
\zeta
satisfy (c1)-(c3).

Proof.

By
Figure

8
, the graded monoid and contraction structures of a
\mathrm{WD}
-algebra depend only on morphisms in
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
, whence the corollary follows immediately.
∎

Since
\cap\in\mathsf{BD}(0,2)
is not a morphism in
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
, algebras for
\mathrm{WD}_{\downarrow}
do not, in general satisfy (e1), and therefore do not have a unit for the induced multiplication
\diamond
(
Figure

8
). For this reason they will be referred to as
non-unital (monochrome) circuit algebras
.

As in
3.18
, let

\mathrm{O}(\infty)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{O}(n)
denote the infinite orthogonal group. This is isomorphic to the the
infinite symplectic group

\mathrm{Sp}(\infty)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{Sp}(n)
. Let
\mathrm{GL}(\infty)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{GL}(n)
denote the infinite general linear group. Since the free
\mathsf{Vect}_{\scriptstyle{\Bbbk}}
-category on
\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}
is the downward Brauer category
\mathsf{Br}_{\downarrow}
(see
3.16
), the following corollary follows immediately from
[
38
]
,
3.16
and
4.15
:

Corollary 4.16
.

There is a canonical inclusion of the category
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathrm{O}(\infty))
of (complex) representations of
\mathrm{O}(\infty)
– which is isomorphic to the category
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathrm{Sp}(\infty))
of representations of
\mathrm{Sp}(\infty)
– as the full subcategory of monochrome non-unital
\mathsf{Vect}_{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}}}
-circuit algebras
\mathcal{A}
such that
A_{0}\cong\Bbbk
, and
\boxtimes_{m,n}\colon A_{m}\otimes A_{n}\to A_{m+n}
is an isomorphism for all
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Likewise, the category
\mathsf{Rep}(\mathrm{GL}(\infty))
of representations of the infinite general linear group describes a full subcategory of the category of monochrome oriented non-unital
\mathsf{Vect}_{\scriptstyle{\mathbb{C}}}
-circuit algebras.

Example 4.17
.

Let
S=(S(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}}
be a collection of sets equipped with an action of
\Sigma
such that, for each
\bm{c}
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
, there is an isomorphism
S(\bm{c})\xrightarrow{\cong}S(\bm{c}\sigma)
. The collection
(FS(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}}
of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured wiring diagrams
decorated by
S
is defined by

\begin{array}[]{ll}FS(\bm{d})&=\coprod_{(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}^{2}\mathfrak{C}}\left(\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})\times\prod_{i=1}^{k}S(\bm{c}_{i})\right)\\
&=\coprod_{((\bm{c}\oplus\dots\oplus\bm{c}_{k}),(f,\lambda)))\in\mathsf{BD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}/\bm{d}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{k}S(\bm{c}_{i})\right)\end{array}.

The set
FS
underlies the
free circuit algebra
\mathcal{F}S
on
S
:

For each
(\overline{f},\lambda)\in\mathrm{WD}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}(\bm{c}_{1},\dots,\bm{c}_{k};\bm{d})
, the morphism
\mathcal{F}S(\overline{f},\lambda)\colon FS(\bm{c}_{1})\times\dots\times FS(\bm{c}_{k})\to FS(\bm{d})
is described by

\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left((\overline{f}^{i},\lambda^{i}),(x_{j_{i}}^{i})_{j_{i}=1}^{m_{i}}\right)\mapsto\left(\gamma\left((\overline{f},\lambda),\left((\overline{f}^{i},\lambda^{i})_{i=1}^{k}\right)\right),(x_{j_{i}}^{i})_{{\overset{1\leq j_{i}\leq m_{i}}{1\leq i\leq k}}}\right).

In general, circuit algebras, like operads (see
[
12
, Section 2.1]
), admit
presentations
in terms of
generators
and
relations
(see
[
10
, Remark 2.6]
). Indeed, every circuit algebra
\mathcal{A}=(A,\alpha)
is obtained as a quotient of the free circuit algebra
\mathcal{F}A
on its underlying coloured collection
A
. Except to state the following familiar example, presentations of circuit algebras are not discussed in this work, and the interested reader is referred to
[
10
]
for more details.

Example 4.18
.

Let
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)=\{\wideparen{+,-}\}
and let
T=T(\bm{c})_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\{+.-\}}
be the
\small{\mathsf{list}}\{+.-\}
-indexed set given such that
T(+,+,-,-)
is the two element set of an
over
- and under-crossing of two oriented intervals embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,1]
, and
T(\bm{c})=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
for
\bm{c}\neq(+,+,-,-)
. The circuit algebra
VT
of virtual oriented tangles (see
[
10
]
) is the quotient of the free circuit algebra
\mathcal{F}T
by relations induced
by the virtual Reidemeister identities (see
[
21
]
).

More generally, we may consider circuit algebras of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured virtual tangles.
This includes, for example, circuit algebras of embedded tangles of mixed dimensions.

5.
Graphical species, modular operads, and circuit operads

By
4.14
, a
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured circuit algebra
\mathcal{A}
in a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{V},\otimes,I)
has the structure of a
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
-graded monoid with contractions and distinguished morphisms
\epsilon_{c}\in\mathcal{V}(I,\mathcal{A}(c,\omega c))
satisfying the conditions (c1)-(c3) and (e1) stated there.

If
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Set}
, then the converse also holds (see
9.9
). A
\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}
-graded set
A=(A(\bm{c}))_{\bm{c}}
, together with morphisms
\epsilon_{c}\colon I\to\mathcal{A}(c,\omega c)
, has the structure of a
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured circuit algebra if and only if it describes a graded monoid
(A,\boxtimes,I)
in
\mathcal{V}
, and is equipped with a family of contraction operations that satisfy the conditions of
4.14
.

Circuit algebras in an arbitrary monoidal category describe enriched version of these structures.

5.1.
Graphical species

Graphical species provide a suitable notion of coloured collection
(\mathcal{A}({\bm{c}}))_{\bm{c}\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathfrak{C}}
for describing various generalisations of (coloured) operads. They were introduced in
[
19
]
to define coloured
\mathsf{Set}
-valued modular operads (called
compact closed categories
in
[
19
]
) in
[
19
,
34
,
14
,
15
]
.

This section provides a short discussion on graphical species in an arbitrary category
\mathsf{E}
with finite limits. For more details, the reader is referred to
[
34
]
, where graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
are discussed at length.

Let
\mathsf{Set_{f}}\subset\mathsf{Set}
denote the full subcategory of finite sets, and let
{\mathbf{B}}\subset\mathsf{Set_{f}}
be the groupoid of finite sets and bijections.

Remark 5.1
.

(See also
Section

2.2
.)

An
\mathsf{E}
-valued presheaf
P\colon{\mathbf{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{E}
, also called a
(monochrome
or
single-sorted) species in
\mathsf{E}

[
20
]
, determines a presheaf
\Sigma^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{E}
by restriction.

Conversely, a
\Sigma
-presheaf
Q
may always be extended to a
{\mathbf{B}}
-presheaf
Q_{{\mathbf{B}}}
, by setting

(5.2)

Q_{{\mathbf{B}}}(X)\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathbf{n},f)\in\Sigma/X}Q(\mathbf{n})\ \text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Let the category
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
be obtained from
{\mathbf{B}}
by adjoining a distinguished object
\lx@sectionsign
that satisfies

•

{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,\lx@sectionsign)=\{id,\tau\}
, and
\tau^{2}=id
;

•

for each finite set
X
and each element
x\in X
, there is a morphism
ch_{x}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,X)
that ‘chooses’
x
, and
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,X)=\{ch_{x}\ ,\ ch_{x}\circ\tau\}_{x\in X}
;

•

for all finite sets
X
and
Y
,
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(X,Y)={\mathbf{B}}(X,Y)
, and morphisms are equivariant with respect to the action of
{\mathbf{B}}
. That is,
ch_{f(x)}=f\circ ch_{x}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,Y)
for all
x\in X
and all bijections
f\colon X\xrightarrow{\cong}Y
.

Definition 5.3
.

A
graphical species
S
in a category
\mathsf{E}
is a presheaf
S\colon{{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{E}
, and
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{E}}}({{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}})
is the category of graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
. When
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Set}
, we write
\mathsf{GS}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{Set}}}
.

Hence, a graphical species
S
is described by an
\mathsf{E}
-valued species
(S_{X})_{X\in{\mathbf{B}}}
and an involutive object
(S_{\lx@sectionsign},S_{\tau})
in
\mathsf{E}
,
together with a family of projections
S(ch_{x})\colon S_{X}\to S_{\lx@sectionsign}
, defined for each finite set
X
, and
x\in X
, and equivariant with respect to the action by
{\mathbf{B}}
.

Henceforth,
\mathsf{E}
will always denote a category with all finite limits.
Let
*
denote the terminal object of
\mathsf{E}
.

Example 5.4
.

The terminal graphical species
{K_{\mathsf{E}}}
is the constant graphical species that sends
\lx@sectionsign
and all finite sets
X
to the terminal object
*
in
\mathsf{E}
.

Definition 5.5
.

A morphism
\gamma\in{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}(S,S^{\prime})
is
colour-preserving
if its component at
\lx@sectionsign
is the identity on
{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}
.
A graphical species
S\in{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
is called monochrome if
S_{\lx@sectionsign}=*
.

Definition 5.6
.

The element category (
2.6
) of a graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{Set}
is denoted by
\mathsf{el}{(S)}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{el}_{{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}}(S)
.

Elements
c\in S_{\lx@sectionsign}
are called
colours of
S
. For each
\underline{c}=(c_{x})_{x\in X}\in{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}^{X}
, the
\underline{c}
-(coloured) arity
{S_{\underline{c}}}
is the fibre above
\underline{c}\in{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}^{X}
of the map
(S(ch_{x}))_{x\in X}\colon{S_{X}}\to{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}^{X}
.

Example 5.7
.

(Compare
3.32
,
4.10
.)
For any palette
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
, the terminal
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured graphical species
{{K}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}
in
\mathsf{Set}
is given by
{{K}}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}_{\underline{c}}=\{*\}
for all
\underline{c}\in\mathfrak{C}^{X}
and all finite sets
X
. This is the terminal object of the category
{\mathsf{GS}^{(\mathfrak{C},\omega)}_{\mathsf{E}}}
of
(\mathfrak{C},\omega)
-coloured graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
, and palette-preserving morphisms.

Recall from Examples
3.32
and
4.10
the category
\mathsf{BD}^{\scriptstyle{{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}}

of directed Brauer diagrams, and the corresponding operad of oriented wiring diagrams and consider the terminal
\{\wideparen{+,-}\}
-coloured
graphical species
K^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}
.

For all finite sets
X
,
K^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}_{X}=\{+,-\}^{X}
is the set of partitions
X=X_{+}\amalg X_{-}
of
X
into
input
and
output
sets, and morphisms
K^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}_{X}\to K^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{\wideparen{+,-}\}}}_{Y}
are bijections
X\xrightarrow{\cong}Y
that preserve the partitions. A
monochrome directed graphical species
is a
\{\wideparen{+,-}\}
-coloured graphical species.

5.2.
Modular operads

This section generalises the definition of modular operads in
\mathsf{Set}
from
[
34
, Section 1.3]
to arbitrary categories with finite limits.

Let
S
be a graphical species in a category
\mathsf{E}
with finite limits. For finite sets
X
and
Y
with elements
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, let
(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{X}\times S_{Y}
denote the pullback

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{x})}
\textstyle{S_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{y}\circ\tau)}
\textstyle{S_{\lx@sectionsign}.}

More generally, given distinct elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in X
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\in Y
, let
(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}
be the limit of the collection
S_{X}\xrightarrow{S(ch_{x_{i}})}S_{\lx@sectionsign}\xleftarrow{S(ch_{y_{i}}\circ\tau)}S_{Y}
,
1\leq i\leq k
.

Definition 5.8
.

A
multiplication

\diamond
on
S
is a family of morphisms

\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}\colon(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}},

in
\mathsf{E}
, defined for all pairs of finite sets
X
and
Y
, with elements
x\in X
and
y\in Y
, and such that

(1)

the obvious diagram

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\quad S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}}}
\textstyle{(S_{Y}\times S_{X})^{y\ddagger x}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{Y,X}^{y\ddagger x}}

commutes in
\mathsf{E}
,

(2)

\diamond
is equivariant with respect to the
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
-action on
S
: if
\hat{\sigma}\colon X\setminus\{x\}\xrightarrow{\cong}W\setminus\{w\}
and
\hat{\rho}\colon Y\setminus\{y\}\xrightarrow{\cong}Z\setminus\{z\}
are restrictions of bijections
\sigma\colon X\xrightarrow{\cong}W
and
\rho\colon Y\xrightarrow{\cong}Z
such that
\sigma(x)=w
and
\rho(y)=z
, then

S(\hat{\sigma}\sqcup\hat{\rho})\ \diamond_{W,Z}^{w\ddagger z}=\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}\ S(\sigma\sqcup\rho),

(where
\sigma\sqcup\rho\colon X\amalg Y\xrightarrow{\cong}W\amalg Z
is the blockwise permutation).

A multiplication
\diamond
on
S
is
unital
if
S
there is a unit-like morphism
\epsilon\in\mathsf{E}(S_{\lx@sectionsign},S_{\mathbf{2}})

such that, for all finite sets
X
and all
x\in X
, the composite

(5.9)

\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(id\times ch_{x})\circ\Delta}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{\lx@sectionsign}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\times\epsilon}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{X,\mathbf{2}}^{x\ddagger 2}}
\textstyle{S_{X}}

is the identity on
S_{X}
.

By (
5.9
) and condition (1) of
5.8
, if a multiplication
\diamond
on
S
admits a unit
\epsilon
, then it is unique.

Let
S
be a graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
, and let
x\neq y
be distinct elements of a finite set
Z
. Let
(S_{Z})^{x\ddagger y}=S_{Z}^{y\ddagger x}
denote the equaliser

(5.10)

\textstyle{S_{Z}^{x\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{x})}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{y}\circ\tau)}
\textstyle{S_{\lx@sectionsign}.}

More generally, for any morphism
f\colon E\to S_{Z}
in
\mathsf{E}
, let
E^{x\ddagger y}=E^{y\ddagger x}
denote the pullback of
f
along the universal map
S_{Z}^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{Z}
.

Given distinct elements
x_{1},y_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{k}
of
S_{Z}
, and a morphism
f\colon E\to Z
in
\mathsf{E}
, then
E^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(E^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k-1}\ddagger y_{k-1}})^{x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}
is the obvious limit.

Invariance of
E^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}
under permutations
(x_{i},y_{i})\mapsto(x_{\sigma i},y_{\sigma i})
,
\sigma\in Aut(\mathbf{k})
follows from invariance of the defining morphisms

\textstyle{E^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},\dots,x_{k}\ddagger y_{k}}_{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{Z-\{x_{1},y_{1},\dots x_{k},y_{k}\}.}}

Definition 5.11
.

A
(graphical species) contraction

\zeta
on
S
is a family of maps
\zeta^{x\ddagger y}_{X}\colon S_{X}^{x\ddagger y}\to S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}
defined for each finite set
X
and pair of distinct elements
x,y\in X
.

The contraction
\zeta
is equivariant with respect to the action of
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
on
S
: If
\hat{\sigma}\colon X\setminus\{x,y\}\xrightarrow{\cong}Z\setminus\{w,z\}
is the restriction of a bijection
\sigma\colon X\xrightarrow{\cong}Z
with
\sigma(x)=w
and
\sigma(y)=z
. Then

(5.12)

S(\hat{\sigma})\circ\zeta^{w\ddagger z}_{Z}=\zeta^{x\ddagger y}_{X}\circ S(\sigma)\colon S_{Z}^{w\ddagger z}\to S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}.

In particular, by (
5.12
), if
\zeta
is a contraction on
S
, then
\zeta_{X}^{x\ddagger y}=\zeta_{X}^{y\ddagger x}
for all finite sets
X
and all pairs of distinct elements
x,y\in X
.

Definition 5.13
.

A modular operad
(S,\diamond,\zeta,\epsilon)
is a graphical species equipped with unital multiplication
(\diamond,\epsilon)
and a contraction
\zeta
such that the following four coherence axioms are satisfied:

(M1)

Multiplication is associative.

For all finite set
X,Y,Z
and elements
x\in X
,
z\in Z
, and distinct
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
,

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y}\times S_{Z})^{x\ddagger y_{1},y_{2}\ddagger z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y_{1}}\times id_{S_{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{id_{S_{X}}\times\diamond_{Y,Z}^{y_{2}\ddagger z}}
\textstyle{\left(S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y_{1}\}}\times S_{Z}\right)^{y_{2}\ddagger z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{y_{2}\ddagger z}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{\left(S_{X}\times S_{(Y\amalg Z)\setminus\{y_{2},z\}}\right)^{x\ddagger y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x\ddagger y_{1}}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y\amalg Z)\setminus\{x,y_{1},y_{2},z\}}.}

(M2)

Contraction satisfies (C2).

(M3)
Multiplication and contraction commute.

For finite sets
X
and
Y
, mutually distinct elements
x_{1},x_{2}
and
x_{3}
in
X
, and
y\in Y
, the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(S_{X}^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}\times S_{Y})^{x_{3}\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}\times id}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x_{3}\ddagger y}}
\textstyle{(S_{X\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}}\times S_{Y)})^{x_{3}\ddagger y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x_{3}\ddagger y}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{3},y\}}^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger x_{2}}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y\}}.}

(M4)

‘Parallel multiplication’ of pairs.

For finite sets
X
and
Y
, and distinct elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
, the following digram commutes:

\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{Y})^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\diamond{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{1},y_{1}\}}^{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}}
\textstyle{=}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{2},y_{2}\}}^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}.}

(M1)
(M3)
(M2)
(M4)

.

5.3.
Circuit Operads

A circuit operad in a category
\mathsf{E}
with finite limits is a graphical species
A
in
\mathsf{E}
that has contraction and graded monoid structures analogous to circuit algebras
(
4.14
).

In this section, it is shown that circuit operads in
\mathsf{E}
canonically admit a modular operad structure. This will be used in Sections
6
-
9.2
, to construct the composite monad for circuit operads by modifying the modular operad monad of

[
34
]
.

Definition 5.14
.

An
external product
on a graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
is given by a
{\mathbf{B}}\times{\mathbf{B}}
-indexed collection of equivariant morphisms

\boxtimes_{X,Y}:S_{X}\times S_{Y}\to S_{X\amalg Y},

in
\mathsf{E}

such that, for all elements
x\in X
, the following diagram commutes in
\mathsf{E}
:

\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch_{x}^{X\amalg Y})\circ\boxtimes_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}
\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S(ch^{X}_{x})}

An
external unit
for the external product
\boxtimes
is a distinguished morphism
\circleddash\colon*\to S_{\mathbf{0}}
such that the composite

\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times*\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\times\circleddash}
\textstyle{S_{X}\times S_{\mathbf{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes}
\textstyle{S_{(X\amalg\mathbf{0})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{X}}

is the identity on
S_{X}
for all finite sets
X
.

Remark 5.15
.

The monoidal unit
\circleddash
for
\boxtimes
is suppressed in the discussion (and notation) since most of the constructions in Sections
5
-
10
of this paper carry through without it.
To agree with circuit algebra conventions, and since most examples of structures related to circuit operads do involve a unital external product, external products in this paper are unital. See also
7.19
, and
[
23
, Paragraph 4.7]
.

Let
\mathsf{CO}^{-}
denote the category of non-unital circuit operads and morphisms of graphical species that preserve the external product and contraction.

A
(unital) circuit operad
is given by a non-unital circuit operad
(S,\boxtimes,\zeta)
together with a unit-like morphism
\epsilon\colon S_{\lx@sectionsign}\to S_{\mathbf{2}}
, such that for all finite sets
X
and all
x\in X
, the composite morphism

(5.20)

\textstyle{S_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(id\times ch_{x})\circ\Delta}
\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{\lx@sectionsign})^{x\ddagger 2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id\times\epsilon}
\textstyle{(S_{X}\times S_{\mathbf{2}})^{x\ddagger 2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boxtimes}
\textstyle{{(S_{X}\boxtimes S_{\mathbf{2}})^{x\ddagger 2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{x\ddagger 2}_{X\amalg\mathbf{2}}}
\textstyle{S_{X}}

is the identity on
S_{X}
. (Here
\Delta\colon S_{X}\to S_{X}\times S_{X}
is the diagonal, and the last map makes use of the canonical isomorphism
(X\amalg\{1\})\setminus\{x\}\cong X
.)

Morphisms in the circuit operad category
\mathsf{CO}
are morphisms in
\mathsf{CO}^{-}
that respect the unit.

The following proposition, that follows immediately from
5.19
and
4.14
, implies that
\mathsf{Set}
-valued circuit algebras describe circuit operads with the obvious product and contraction:

Proposition 5.21
.

There is a canonical faithful functor
{\mathsf{CA}}\to\mathsf{CO}
.

In fact, we have the following proposition, that will follow from
7.16
and
9.4
, together with the classical theory of distributive laws
[
2
]
:

Proposition 5.22
.

There are canonical monadic adjunctions

(5.23)

\textstyle{{\mathsf{CO}}^{-}_{\mathsf{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{CO}}_{\mathsf{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{\vdash}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{MO}}^{-}_{\mathsf{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptsize{\top}}
\textstyle{{\mathsf{MO}}_{\mathsf{E}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

between the categories of (non-unital) modular and product modular operads in
\mathsf{E}
.

The right adjoints of the pairs
{\mathsf{MO}}_{\mathsf{E}}\rightleftarrows{\mathsf{CO}}_{\mathsf{E}}
(and
{\mathsf{MO}}^{-}_{\mathsf{E}}\rightleftarrows{\mathsf{CO}}^{-}_{\mathsf{E}}
) in
5.22
are induced by the forgetful functor
(S,\boxtimes,\zeta)\mapsto(S,\diamond,\zeta)
where the multiplication
\diamond
is defined by suitable compositions
\zeta\circ\boxtimes
as in (
5.16
). (See also
Figure

8
.)

The left adjoint is induced by the free graded monoid monad. If
S
is a graphical species, then there is a graphical species
LS
defined by

LS_{X}=\mathrm{colim}_{(Y,f)\in\mathrm{Core}(X/\mathsf{Set_{f}})}\coprod_{y\in Y}S_{f^{-1}(y)}

where
\mathrm{Core}(X/\mathsf{Set_{f}})
is the maximal subgroupoid of the slice category
X/\mathsf{Set_{f}}
whose objects are morphisms of finite sets
f\colon X\to Y
, and whose morphisms
(Y,f)\to(Y^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are isomorphisms
g\cong Y\to Y^{\prime}
such that
gf=f^{\prime}
.

The free monoid structure induces the external product on
LS
, and, if
(S,\diamond,\zeta)
is a (non-unital) modular operad, then the modular operad axioms imply that the pair of operations
\zeta
and
\diamond
, define a contraction on
LS
. (See
Section

9.2
.)

In particular, since units for the modular operadic multiplication are unique:

Corollary 5.24
.

The canonical forgetful functors
{\mathsf{CO}}_{\mathsf{E}}\to{\mathsf{CO}}^{-}_{\mathsf{E}}
and
\mathsf{MO}\to\mathsf{MO}^{-}
are inclusions of categories.

In
Section

9.2
, we shall see that the converse to
5.21
is also true, and there is an equivalence of categories
{\mathsf{CA}}\simeq\mathsf{CO}
(
9.9
).

Example 5.25
.

(Recall Examples
3.32
,
4.10
and
5.7
.)
By
[
9
]
, wheeled PROPs are equivalent to circuit algebras. Hence, by
5.21
, wheeled PROPs in
\mathsf{Set}
describe
\mathsf{Set}
-valued circuit operads.

The image of a wheeled PROP under the forgetful functor
\mathsf{CO}\to\mathsf{MO}
is its underlying
wheeled properad
(see
[
13
,
47
]
).

6.
Background on graphs

The combinatorics of modular operads have been fully described in
[
34
, Section 8]
in terms of a category
\Xi
of graphs. The remainder of the present paper is concerned with an analogous construction for circuit operads.

This section reviews the definition of
the category
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
of graphs and étale morphisms – introduced in
[
19
]
, and used in
[
23
,
14
,
15
,
34
]
– and describes how the graphs in this category are related to wiring diagrams.
The interested reader is referred to
[
34
, Sections 3 & 4]
for more details and explicit proofs related to the graphical formalism.

6.1.
The étale category of graphs

Recall (from
3.3
) that
\mathbf{n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\{1,\dots,n\}
. Let
(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)\in\small{\mathsf{list}}(\mathbb{N})\times\mathbb{N}
.

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathrm{WD}(m_{1},\dots,m_{k};n)
be a monochrome wiring diagram with underlying (open) Brauer diagram
f=(\tau_{f},0)\in\mathsf{BD}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}m_{i},n)
where
\tau_{f}
is a pairing on the set
\partial f=\mathbf{n}\amalg(\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{m}_{i})
.

Then
\overline{f}
may equivalently be written as

(6.1)

\textstyle{\partial f}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{f}}
\textstyle{{\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{m}_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
inc
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathbf{k}}

where
p\colon\coprod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{m}_{i}\to\mathbf{k}
is the canonical projection.

By forgetting the orderings on
\mathbf{k}
and each
\mathbf{m}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq k
, we obtain the definition, originally due to Joyal and Kock
[
19
]
, of a (Feynman) graph.

Definition 6.2
.

A
graph

\mathcal{G}
is a diagram of finite sets

such that
s\colon H\to E
is injective, and
\tau\colon E\to E
is an involution without fixed points.

Elements of
V
are vertices of
\mathcal{G}
and elements of
E
are called
edges
of
\mathcal{G}
. The set
\tilde{E}
of
\tau
-orbits in
E
, where
\tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}
is the orbit of an edge
e\in E
, is the set of connections in
\mathcal{G}
. Elements
h
of the set
H
– of
half-edges
of
\mathcal{G}
– will also be written as ordered pairs
h=(s(h),t(h))\in E\times V
.

Remark 6.4
.

By
3.20
, a graph is a rule for gluing closed intervals along their boundaries. The geometric realisation of a graph
\mathcal{G}
is the one-dimensional space
|\mathcal{G}|
, obtained by taking the discrete space
\{*_{v}\}_{v\in V}
, and, for each
e\in E
, a copy
[0,\frac{1}{2}]_{e}
of the interval
[0,\frac{1}{2}]
subject to the identifications

•

0_{s(h)}\sim*_{t(h)}
for
h\in H
,

•

(\frac{1}{2})_{e}\sim(\frac{1}{2})_{\tau e}
for all
e\in E
.

Definition 6.5
.

An
inner edge
of
\mathcal{G}
is an element
e\in E
such that
\{e,\tau e\}\subset im(s)
. The set
{E_{\bullet}}\subset E
of inner edges of
\mathcal{G}
is the maximal subset of
im(s)\subset E
that is closed under
\tau
. The set of
inner
\tau
-orbits

\tilde{e}\in\widetilde{E}
with
e\in{E_{\bullet}}
is denoted by
\widetilde{{E_{\bullet}}}
.
Elements of the set
E_{0}=E\setminus im(s)
are
ports
of
\mathcal{G}
.

A
stick component
of a graph
\mathcal{G}
is a pair
\{e,\tau e\}
of edges of
\mathcal{G}
such that
e
and
\tau e
are both ports.

A graph
\mathcal{G}
that is obtained, as in (
6.1
), from a wiring diagram in
\mathrm{WD}
is called
ordered
.

.

For any set

, let

Example 6.7
.

(See also
Figure

13
(d).) The
X
-corolla

{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
associated to a finite set
X
has the form

\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}_{X}}:}
\textstyle{X\amalg X^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\dagger}
\textstyle{X^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
inc
\textstyle{\{*\}.}

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with vertex and edge sets
V
and
E
respectively. For each vertex
v
, define
\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}t^{-1}(v)\subset H
to be the fibre of
t
at
v
, and let
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}s(\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}})\subset E
.

Definition 6.8
.

Edges in the set
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}
are said to be
incident on
v
.

The map
|\cdot|\colon V\to\mathbb{N}
,
v\mapsto|v|\overset{\textup{def}}{=}|\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}}|
, defines the
valency
of
v
and
V_{n}\subset V
is the set of
n
-valent
vertices of
\mathcal{G}
.

A vertex
v
is
bivalent
if
|v|=2
. An
isolated vertex
of
\mathcal{G}
is a vertex
v\in V(\mathcal{G})
such that
|v|=0
. A
bivalent graph
is a graph
\mathcal{G}
with
V=V_{2}
.

Bivalent and isolated vertices are particularly important in
Section

8.3
.

Vertex valency induces an
\mathbb{N}
-grading on the edge set
E
(and half-edge set
H
) of
\mathcal{G}
: For
n\geq 1
, define
H_{n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}t^{-1}(V_{n})
and
E_{n}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}s(H_{n})
. Since
s(H)=E\setminus E_{0}=\coprod_{n\geq 1}E_{n}
,

E=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}E_{n}.

Example 6.9
.

The stick graph
(\shortmid)
(
6.6
) has
E_{0}(\shortmid)=E(\shortmid)=\mathbf{2}
, and
{E_{\bullet}}(\shortmid)=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
. Conversely, the wheel graph
\mathcal{W}
has
E_{0}(\mathcal{W})=\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
and
{E_{\bullet}}(W)=E_{2}(\mathcal{W})=E(\mathcal{W})\cong\mathbf{2}.

For all finite sets
X
, the
X
-corolla
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
(see
6.7
) with vertex
*
has set of ports
E_{0}({\mathcal{C}_{X}})=X
and
\nicefrac{{E}}{{*}}=X^{\dagger}
. If
X=\mathbf{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then
|*|=n
, so
V=V_{n}
, and
E=E_{n}\amalg E_{0}
with
E_{i}\cong X
for
i=0,n
.

Let
\mathsf{\bm{D}}
be the small category
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bullet\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bullet}
. We are interested in the subcategory
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
of
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{\bm{D}})
whose objects are graphs and whose morphisms are described by:

Definition 6.10
.

An
étale morphism

f\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
of graphs is a commuting diagram of finite sets

(6.11)

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\prime}}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}}

such that the right-hand square is a pullback. The category of graphs and étale morphisms is denoted by
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

It is straightforward to verify that the right hand square in (
6.11
) is a pullback if and only if, for all
v\in V
, the map
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\to\nicefrac{{E^{\prime}}}{{f(v)}}
induced by the restriction of the map
V\to V^{\prime}
is bijective. Hence, étale morphisms describe local isomorphisms, as the name suggests.

Example 6.12
.

For any graph
\mathcal{G}
with edge set
E
, there is a canonical (up to unique isomorphism) bijection
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\shortmid,\mathcal{G})\cong E
. The morphism
1\mapsto e
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\shortmid,\mathcal{G})
that
chooses
an edge
e
is denoted
ch_{e}
, or
ch^{\mathcal{G}}_{e}
.

Pointwise disjoint union defines a symmetric strict monoidal structure on
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
with unit given by the empty graph
\oslash\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\ \hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\ }}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 34.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.50002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.50002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 79.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ \hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces
.

Definition 6.13
.

A graph is
connected
if it cannot be written as a disjoint union of at least two non-empty graphs. A connected component
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{G}
is a maximal connected subdiagram of
\mathcal{G}
.

In particular, any graph
\mathcal{G}
is the disjoint union of its connected components, and the inclusion
\mathcal{H}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
of a connected component
\mathcal{H}
of
\mathcal{G}
describes a pointwise injective étale morphism. The empty graph
\oslash
is not connected.

Disjoint union commutes with geometric realisation: for all graphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
,
|\mathcal{G}\amalg\mathcal{H}|=|\mathcal{G}|\amalg|\mathcal{H}|
, and a graph
\mathcal{G}
is connected if and only if it’s geometric realisation
|\mathcal{G}|
is a connected space.

Example 6.14
.

Let
X
and
Y
be finite sets. Write
\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
for the disjoint union of
X
and
Y
-corollas:

\textstyle{(X\amalg Y)\amalg(X\amalg Y)^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\dagger}
\textstyle{(X\amalg Y)^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
inc
\scriptstyle{t_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{\{*_{X},*_{Y}\}}

where the arrow
t_{X,Y}
is the obvious projection
X^{\dagger}\to*_{X}
,
Y^{\dagger}\to*_{Y}
. This has ports
X\amalg Y=E_{0}({\mathcal{C}_{X}})\amalg E_{0}({\mathcal{C}_{Y}})
and no internal edges. The canonical inclusions
\iota_{X}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\to\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}\leftarrow{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}\colon\iota_{Y}
are étale.

Example 6.15
.

Up to isomorphism,
(\shortmid)
is the only connected graph with no vertices.
As in
[
34
]
(and following
[
22
]
) a
shrub

\mathcal{S}
is a disjoint union of stick graphs. Any commuting diagram of the form (
6.11
) where
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}
is a shrub trivially satisfies the pullback condition, and hence defines a morphism in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

Example 6.16
.

The pair of parallel morphisms
id,\tau\colon(\shortmid)\rightrightarrows(\shortmid)
given by the endomorphisms of
(\shortmid)
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
has coequaliser
*\leftarrow\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}\rightarrow\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}
in
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{\bm{D}})
. Since this is not a graph, the morphisms
id_{(\shortmid)}
and
\tau
do not admit a coequaliser in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
. This provided the key motivating example for
[
34
]
.

The two endomorphisms
id_{\mathcal{W}},\tau_{\mathcal{W}}\colon\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}
, viewed as morphisms of presheaves on
\mathsf{\bm{D}}
, have coequaliser
*\leftarrow*\rightarrow*
which is terminal in
\mathsf{psh}(\mathsf{\bm{D}})
, and is not a graph. In particular,
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
does not have a terminal object and is therefore not closed under (finite) limits.

As
6.16
shows,
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
does not, in general, admit finite colimits. However, if
\mathcal{G}
is a graph and
e_{1},e_{2}\in E_{0}
are ports of
\mathcal{G}
with
e_{1}\neq\tau e_{2}
, then the colimit
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}
of the parallel morphisms

(6.17)

ch_{e_{1}},ch_{e_{2}}\circ\tau\colon(\shortmid)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G}

is the graph described by identifying
e_{1}\sim\tau e_{2}
and
e_{2}\sim\tau e_{1}
in the diagram (
6.3
) defining
\mathcal{G}
.

Example 6.18
.

The graph
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}^{e\ddagger\tau e}
obtained by identifying the ports of the
\mathbf{2}
-corolla is isomorphic to the wheel graph
\mathcal{W}
.

Example 6.19
.

More generally, for
X
a finite set with distinct elements
x
and
y
, the graph
\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}
has ports in
E_{0}=X\setminus\{x,y\}
, one inner
\tau
-orbit
\{x,y\}
(bold-face in
Figure

14
), and one vertex
v
:

\textstyle{(X\setminus\{x,y\})\amalg X^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{X^{\dagger}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\{v\},}

where
\tau x^{\dagger}=y^{\dagger}
and
\tau z=z^{\dagger}
for
z\in X\setminus\{x,y\}
.
Graphs of the form
\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}
encode formal contractions in graphical species.

Since the monoidal structure on
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
is induced by the cocartesian monoidal structure on diagrams of finite sets, colimits in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
, when they exist, commute with
\amalg
. In particular,
for any graphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
and distinct ports
e_{1},e_{2}
of
\mathcal{G}
,

(6.20)

\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}\amalg\mathcal{H}=(\mathcal{G}\amalg\mathcal{H})^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}.

Example 6.21
.

In particular, recall that
\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}
is the disjoint union
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
. Let
x\in X
, and

. By identifying the edges
x\sim\tau y,y\sim\tau x
, we obtain a graph
\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\left({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}\right)^{x\ddagger y}
, that has two vertices and one inner edge orbit

, highlighted in bold-face in

.

\textstyle{\left(\small{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}\amalg(X\amalg Y)^{\dagger}}\right)}

with

the obvious inclusion and the involution

z\leftrightarrow z^{\dagger}
for

. The map

t^{-1}(v_{X})=\left(X\setminus\{x\}\right)^{\dagger}\amalg\{y\}
and

.

In the construction of modular operads, graphs of the form
\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}
are used to encode formal multiplications
in graphical species.

x

|

|

In general, given ports
e_{1},e_{2}
of a graph
\mathcal{G}
such that
e_{1}\neq\tau e_{2}
, then
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}
is formed by ‘gluing’ the ports
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
(see
Figure

14
). As the following examples shows, the result of forming
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}
is somewhat surprising when the ports
e_{1}=\tau e_{2}
are the edges of a stick component:

Example 6.22
.

Observe that,

ch_{1}^{(\shortmid)}\colon(\shortmid)\to(\shortmid)
,
1\mapsto 1
is the identity on
(\shortmid)
and
ch_{2}^{(\shortmid)}\colon(\shortmid)\to(\shortmid)
,
1\mapsto 2
is precisely
\tau
. Hence

ch_{2}^{(\shortmid)}\circ\tau=\tau^{2}=id_{\shortmid},

and therefore

(\shortmid)^{1\ddagger 2}=(\shortmid).

It follows that, if a pair of ports
e_{1},e_{2}=\tau e_{1}\in E_{0}(\mathcal{G})
form a stick component of a graph
\mathcal{G}
(
6.5
), then
\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e_{2}}\cong\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 6.23
.

A graph
embedding
is a morphism
f\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
that factors as

\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e^{\prime}_{1},\dots,e_{k}\dagger e^{\prime}_{k}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}

where the first morphism describes a colimit
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{e_{1}\ddagger e^{\prime}_{1},\dots,e_{k}\dagger e^{\prime}_{k}}
of pairs of parallel maps

ch_{e_{i}},ch_{e^{\prime}_{i}}\circ\tau\colon(\shortmid)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{G},

(with
e_{1},e^{\prime}_{1},\dots,e_{k},e^{\prime}_{k}
distinct ports of
\mathcal{G}
), and the second morphism is an inclusion (pointwise injection) in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\amalg\mathcal{S}
, where
\mathcal{S}
is a shrub and each connected component of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
has non-empty vertex set. The following follows immediately from the definition of embeddings.

Lemma 6.24
.

A morphism
f\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H})
is an embedding if and only if:

•

the images
f(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
and
f(\mathcal{S})
are disjoint in
\mathcal{H}
;

•

f
is injective on vertices and half edges;

•

the restriction of
f
to
\mathcal{S}
is injective.

Embeddings form a subcategory in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

Remark 6.25
.

The name ‘embedding’ comes from
[
14
,
15
]
. In
[
34
, Proposition 4.8]
, I mistakenly identified the graph morphisms satisfying the conditions of
6.24
with the monomorphisms in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
, and used the term ‘monomorphism’ to refer to ‘embeddings’ throughout the paper. Whilst all monomorphisms are embeddings, the converse does not hold, as explained in
[
14
, Section 1.3]
(see, in particular, their Example 1.23). The last part of the given proof of
[
34
, Proposition 4.8]
holds only when we consider parallel morphisms from a graph with no stick components.

As a consequence, statements about graph ‘monomorphisms’ in
[
34
]
should actually refer to graph ‘embeddings’. The substance is unchanged.

.

For
k\geq 0
, the
line graph

\mathcal{L}^{k}
is the connected bivalent graph (illustrated in

) with ports in

6.2.
The element category of a graph

Intuitively, a graph may be thought of as a collection of corollas glued together along inner edges. This idea is made precise by the element category
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
of a graph
\mathcal{G}
.

Observe first that
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\shortmid,\shortmid)\cong{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,\lx@sectionsign)
canonically, so the maps
\lx@sectionsign\mapsto(\shortmid)
and
X\mapsto{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
describe a full inclusion of categories
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.
The restriction of the Yoneda embedding to the image of
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
in
\mathsf{GS}
induces a functor
\Upsilon\colon\mathsf{Gr_{et}}\to\mathsf{GS}
, given by

(6.28)

\Upsilon\mathcal{G}\colon\mathcal{C}\longmapsto\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{G})\text{ for }\mathcal{C}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}.

Definition 6.29
.

The
element category

\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
of a graph
\mathcal{G}
is the category of elements
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{el}{(\Upsilon\mathcal{G})}\cong{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}/\mathcal{G}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with edge set
E
. There is a maximal shrub
\mathcal{S}(E)\subset\mathcal{G}
:

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
∅
∅
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{V}

whose connected components are indexed by the set of orbits
\tilde{E}
of
\tau
in
E
. For each orbit
\tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}
, let
\iota_{{\tilde{e}}}\colon({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
be the inclusion of the stick graph
\textstyle{\{e,\tau e\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
∅
\textstyle{\hbox{\cmsy\char 59\relax}.}

Recall that, for each
v\in V
,
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}s(t^{-1}(v))
is the set of edges incident on
v
. Let
\mathbf{v}=(\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}})^{\dagger}
denote its formal involution. Then the corolla
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}
is given by

{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}=\qquad\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
