The inclusion
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\hookrightarrow E
induces a morphism
\iota_{{v}}^{\mathcal{G}}
or
\iota_{{v}}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\to\mathcal{G}
.
Observe that, whenever there is an edge
e
such that
e
and
\tau e
are both incident on the same vertex
v
– so
\{e,\tau e\}
forms a
loop
at
v
– then
\iota_{{v}}
is not injective on edges.

If
\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}
is empty – so
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}
is an isolated vertex – then
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
is a connected component of
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 6.30
.

A
neighbourhood
of a vertex
v\in V
is an embedding
u\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
such that
\iota_{{v}}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\to\mathcal{G}
factors through
u
.

Definition 6.31
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph. An
essential morphism
(or
essential subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
) is a morphism of the form
\iota_{{\tilde{e}}}\colon({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\to\mathcal{G}
(
\tilde{e}\in\widetilde{E}
) or
\iota_{{v}}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\to\mathcal{G}
(
v\in V
).
The full subcategory
\mathsf{es}{(\mathcal{G})}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr_{et}}/\mathcal{G}
on the
essential subgraphs is called the
essential category of
\mathcal{G}
.

Non-identity morphisms in
\mathsf{es}{(\mathcal{G})}
are in canonical bijection with half-edges of
\mathcal{G}
.

By definition,
\mathsf{es}{(\mathcal{G})}(\iota_{{\tilde{e}}},\iota_{{v}})\subset\mathsf{Gr_{et}}({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}},{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}})
for all edges
e
and vertices
v
of
\mathcal{G}
. If

h=(e,v)\in H
, then
e\in E({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\cap E({\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}})
, and
\delta_{{h}}\colon\iota_{{\tilde{e}}}\to\iota_{{v}}
denotes the (unique) morphism in
\mathsf{es}{(\mathcal{G})}
that fixes
e
.

The following is proved in
[
34
, Lemma 4.17 & Lemma 4.25]
:

Lemma 6.32
.

There is an equivalence of categories
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\simeq\mathsf{es}{(\mathcal{G})}
and, for all graphs
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{colim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{es}{(\mathcal{G})}}\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{colim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}}\mathcal{C},

canonically. It follows that the embedding
\Upsilon\colon\mathsf{Gr_{et}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{GS}
of (
6.28
) is fully faithful.

In particular, if
\mathcal{G}
has no stick components, then, for each
\tilde{e}\in\widetilde{{E_{\bullet}}}
, the essential morphism
\iota_{{\tilde{e}}}:({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
factors in precisely two ways through the canonical morphism
\coprod_{v\in V}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\to\mathcal{G}
that restricts to
\iota_{{v}}
on each
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}
:

(6.33)

\textstyle{({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(e,\tau e)\mapsto(e,e^{\dagger})}
\scriptstyle{(e,\tau e)\mapsto((\tau e)^{\dagger},\tau e)}
\textstyle{\coprod_{v\in V}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}.}

Hence, there exist parallel morphisms
\coprod_{\tilde{e}\in\widetilde{{E_{\bullet}}}}({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\rightrightarrows\coprod_{v\in V}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}
such that the diagram

(6.34)

\textstyle{\coprod_{\tilde{e}\in\widetilde{{E_{\bullet}}}}({\shortmid_{\tilde{e}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coprod_{v\in V}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}}

describes a coequaliser in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
. Of course, the pair of parallel morphisms in (
6.34
) is not unique (there are
2^{|\widetilde{{E_{\bullet}}}|-1}
choices), but they are unique up to isomorphism.

6.3.
Evaluating graphical species on graphs

By
[
31
, Section VII.2]
, the inclusion of presheaves
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{E}}}(\mathsf{Gr_{et}})
:

(6.35)

S\longmapsto\left(\mathcal{G}\mapsto\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}}S(\mathcal{C})\right),

induced by the fully faithful inclusion
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
, is also fully faithful. Henceforth, the same notation will be used for graphical species viewed as presheaves on
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
and for their image as presheaves on
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph. A family of morphisms
\mathfrak{U}=\{f_{i}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{G})\}_{i\in I}
is
jointly surjective
on
\mathcal{G}
if
\mathcal{G}=\bigcup_{i\in I}im(f_{i})
.
Families of jointly surjective morphisms on
\mathcal{G}

define the covers at
\mathcal{G}
for a canonical
étale topology

J
on
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
. Since
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
refines every cover at
\mathcal{G}
, the category
\mathsf{GS}
of graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
is equivalent to the category
\mathsf{sh}(\mathsf{Gr_{et}},J)
of étale sheaves on
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
. It follows, in particular, that
for all graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{Set}
, and all graphs
\mathcal{G}
, there is a canonical isomorphism

S(\mathcal{G})\cong\mathsf{GS}(\Upsilon\mathcal{G},S)
(see
[
34
, Section 4.4]
) .

Definition 6.36
.

If
S
is a graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
, an element
\alpha\in S(\mathcal{G})
is called an
S
-structure on
\mathcal{G}
.
Objects of the category
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(S)
, of
S
-structured graphs
, are pairs
(\mathcal{G},\alpha)
with
\alpha\in S(\mathcal{G})
, and morphisms
(\mathcal{G},\alpha)\to(\mathcal{G}^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(S)
are given by morphisms
f\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
such that
S(f)(\alpha^{\prime})=\alpha
.

Example 6.37
.

Let
S
be a graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
. By (
6.35
),

S(\mathcal{G})\times S(\mathcal{H})=S(\mathcal{G}\amalg\mathcal{H}),

for any graphs
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{H}
. In particular, for finite sets
X
and
Y
,

(6.38)

S_{X}\times S_{Y}=S({\mathcal{C}_{X}})\times S({\mathcal{C}_{Y}})=S({\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}).

Example 6.39
.

Let
X
be a finite set with distinct elements
x
and
y
. Recall from (
5.10
) that
S_{X}^{x\ddagger y}\in\mathsf{E}
is the equaliser of the morphisms
S(ch_{x}),S(ch_{y}\circ\tau)\colon S_{X}\to S_{\lx@sectionsign}
. Since the graph
\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}
is defined as the coequaliser of the morphisms
ch_{x},ch_{y}\circ\tau\colon(\shortmid)\to{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
(see
6.19
), it follows from (
6.35
) that

(6.40)

S^{x\ddagger y}_{X}=S(\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y}).

So, a contraction
\zeta
on a graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
is defined by a collection of maps
S(\mathcal{C}^{x\ddagger y}_{X})\to S_{X\setminus\{x,y\}}
where
X
is a finite set with distinct elements
x,y\in X
.

7.
Non-unital circuit operads

Henceforth, it will always be assumed that the category
\mathsf{E}
has sufficient limits and colimits for all given constructions.

This section gives a short outline, closely following
[
19
]
and
[
34
, Section 5]
, of the construction of the non-unital circuit operad monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
.

7.1.
Gluing constructions and labelled graphs

In
Section

7.2
, the monad
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
whose algebras are non-unital circuit operads will be defined in terms of graphs. In
Section

9.2
,
9.9
will establish an equivalence between the categories
{\mathsf{CA}}
and
\mathsf{CO}
of circuit algebras and circuit operads in
\mathsf{Set}
. It follows that, since circuit algebras are algebras for operads of wiring diagrams (
4.9
), there must be graph constructions that are analogous to operadic composition in
\mathrm{WD}
.

The following terminology is based on
[
22
]
:

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph. A
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
is a functor
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
(or
\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathcal{G}}
) such that

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbf{\Gamma}(a)=(\shortmid)&\text{ for all }(\shortmid,a)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})},\\
E_{0}(\mathbf{\Gamma}(b))=X&\text{ for all }({\mathcal{C}_{X}},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})},\end{array}

and, for all
(\mathcal{C}_{X_{b}},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
and all
x\in X_{b}
,

\mathbf{\Gamma}(ch_{x})=ch^{\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)}_{x}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\shortmid,\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)).

A
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
is
non-degenerate
if, for all
v\in V
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}})
has no stick components. Otherwise,
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is called
degenerate
.

In particular, the empty
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}
-indexed graph of graphs
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\mapsto\oslash
is non-degenerate.

Figure 16.
A
\mathcal{G}
shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
describes
graph substitution
in which each vertex
v
of
\mathcal{G}
is replaced by a graph
\mathcal{G}_{v}
according to a bijection
E_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{v})\xrightarrow{\cong}(\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}})^{\dagger}
. When
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is non-degenerate, taking its colimit corresponds to erasing the inner (blue) nesting.

Informally, a non-degenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs may be viewed as a rule for substituting graphs into vertices of
\mathcal{G}
as in
Figure

16
.

Proposition 7.2
.

A

\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
admits a colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
, and, for each
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
, the universal map
b_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\colon\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)\to{\mathbf{\Gamma}}({\mathcal{G}})
is an embedding in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is non-degenerate and
\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)\neq\oslash
for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
, then

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
describes a bijection
E({\mathbf{\Gamma}}({\mathcal{G}}))\cong E(\mathcal{G})\amalg\coprod_{v\in V}{E_{\bullet}}(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}}))
of sets;

•

\mathbf{\Gamma}
induces an identity
E_{0}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{=}E_{0}({\mathbf{\Gamma}}({\mathcal{G}}))
of ports;

•

there is a canonical surjective map
V(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G}))\twoheadrightarrow V(\mathcal{G})
induced by the embeddings
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}})\colon\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}})\to\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
for each vertex
v
of
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is non-degenerate, then
\mathbf{\Gamma}
admits a colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
by
[
34
, Proposition 5.16]
. In case
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is degenerate, the existence of a colimit in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
follows from
[
34
, Proposition 7.15]
.

The other statements follow directly from
[
34
, Corollary 5.19]
.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with
n
ports,
k
vertices, and such that each vertex
v_{i}\in V
has
m_{i}=|\nicefrac{{E}}{{v_{i}}}|
adjacent edges. Up to ordering,
\mathcal{G}
describes a wiring diagram
\overline{g}\in\mathrm{WD}(\mathbf{m}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{m}_{k};\mathbf{n})
with no closed components, and a
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)\neq\oslash
for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
describes a choice of
k
wiring diagrams
\overline{f}^{i}\in\mathrm{WD}(\bm{l}_{1},\dots,\bm{l}_{k_{i}};\mathbf{m}_{i})
for
(l_{1},\dots,l_{k_{i}})\in\small{\mathsf{list}}\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq i\leq k
.

Non-degeneracy of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
corresponds to the condition, discussed in
3.22
, that, for each of the
\overline{f}^{i}
, the underlying morphism
f^{i}
of Brauer diagrams is a morphism of the downward Brauer category
f^{i}\in\mathsf{BD}_{\downarrow}(l_{1}+\dots+l_{k_{i}},m_{i})
. This means, informally, that
f^{i}
does not contain caps. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is non-degenerate, then its colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
is described by the composition
\gamma\left(\overline{g},(\overline{f}_{i})_{i}\right)
in
\mathrm{WD}
. (Colimits of degenerate graphs of graphs are discussed in
Section

8.3
.)

In both the non-degenerate and degenerate cases, the colimit of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is induced by the pushout diagram (
3.23
).
7.2
may also be proved using this construction.

Example 7.4
.

By
6.32
, every graph
\mathcal{G}
is the colimit of the (non-degenerate)
identity
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs

\mathbf{I}^{\mathcal{G}}
given by the forgetful functor
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
,
(\mathcal{C},b)\mapsto\mathcal{C}
. It follows from (5.9) in
[
34
, Section 5.2]
that, if
\mathcal{G}
has no stick components, this is equivalent to the statement that
\mathcal{G}
is the coequaliser of the canonical diagram

(7.5)

\textstyle{\mathcal{S}({E_{\bullet}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coprod_{v\in V}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigcup(\iota_{{v}})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}.}

The identity
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs corresponds, as in
7.3
to a composition of wiring diagrams of the form
\gamma\left(\overline{g},(\overline{id}_{m_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}\right)
.

Definition 7.6
.

The category of
non-degenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graphs of graphs
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{(\mathcal{G})}
is the full subcategory of non-degenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graphs of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}:\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
in the category of functors
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
and natural transformations.

Definition 7.7
.

Let
X
be a finite set. A generalised
X
-graph
(henceforth
X
-graph) is a
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}
-shaped graph of graphs. An
X
-graph is
admissible
if it is non-degenerate.

The groupoid of
(admissible)
X
-graphs
and
X
-isomorphisms
is the maximal subgroupoid
\mathrm{Core}(\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{({\mathcal{C}_{X}})})\subset\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{({\mathcal{C}_{X}})}
and is denoted by
X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
.

More generally, for any graph
\mathcal{G}
and any
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
,
[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
denotes the groupoid of non-degenerate
\mathcal{C}
-shaped graphs of graphs and isomorphisms.

Hence, a generalised
X
-graph is given by a pair
\mathcal{X}=(\mathcal{G},\rho)
, where
\mathcal{G}
is a graph and
\rho\colon E_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}X
. An
X
-graph is
admissible
if it has no stick components, and an
X
-isomorphism in
X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}^{\prime})
is an isomorphism
g\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
that preserves the
X
-labelling:
\rho^{\prime}\circ g_{E_{0}}=\rho\colon E_{0}\to X.

Example 7.8
.

For any graph
\mathcal{G}
, a (non-degenerate)
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
is precisely a choice of (admissible)
X_{b}
-graph
\mathcal{X}_{b}
for each
({\mathcal{C}_{X_{b}}},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
.

Example 7.9
.

If
X
and
Y
are finite sets, then the disjoint union of corollas
\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}={\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
is an admissible
X\amalg Y
-graph.

Let
S
be a graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
, and
\mathcal{G}
a graph. If
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
is a non-degenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs with colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
, then the composition

\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{\mathrm{op}}\xrightarrow{S}\mathsf{E}

defines a functor
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{E}
with limit
S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G}))
.

Example 7.10
.

Recall
6.36
. If
S
is a graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathcal{G}
is any graph, then a non-degenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
and choice of
S
-structure
\alpha\in S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G}))
determines a functor
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{S}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(S)
by
b\mapsto S(b_{\mathbf{\Gamma}})(\alpha)
(where, for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
,
b_{\mathbf{\Gamma}}
is the universal map described in
7.2
). In this case
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{S}
is called a
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of
S
-structured graphs
.

7.2.
A monad for non-unital circuit operads

It is now straightforward to construct a monad
on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
, whose algebras are non-unital circuit operads in
\mathsf{E}
.

Let
{T^{\times}}={T^{\times}}_{\mathsf{E}}\colon{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}\to{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
be the endofunctor defined, for all
S\in{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
, by

(7.11)

\begin{array}[]{llll}{T^{\times}}S_{\lx@sectionsign}&=&S_{\lx@sectionsign},&\\
{T^{\times}}S_{X}&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}}S(\mathcal{X})&\text{ for all finite sets }X\end{array}

On isomorphisms in
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
,
{T^{\times}}S
acts by relabelling graph ports. To see that
{T^{\times}}S
extends to all morphisms in
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
and therefore defines a graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
, let
g\colon\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
be a port-preserving isomorphism in
X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
, and for each
x\in X
, let
ch_{x}^{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\shortmid,\mathcal{G})
be the map
ch_{\rho^{-1}(x)}
defined by
1\mapsto\rho^{-1}(x)\in E_{0}(\mathcal{G})
.
Then,

S(g\circ ch^{\mathcal{X}}_{x})=S(ch^{\mathcal{X}}_{x})S(g)=S(ch^{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}_{x}).

Hence, the projections
{T^{\times}}S(ch_{x})\colon{T^{\times}}S_{X}\to S_{\lx@sectionsign}
induced by

S({ch^{\mathcal{X}}_{x}})\colon S(\mathcal{X})\to S(\shortmid)=S_{\lx@sectionsign}
,
are well-defined. The assignment
S\mapsto{T^{\times}}S
is clearly natural on
S\in{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
, and so
{T^{\times}}
defines an endofunctor on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
.

Lemma 7.12
.

For any graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
and any finite set
\mathcal{X}
, there is a canonical isomorphism

({{T^{\times}}})^{2}S_{X}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\right),

where, as usual,

\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})
is the colimit of the
\mathcal{X}
-shaped graph of graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{(\mathcal{X})}
.

Proof.

By definition,

\begin{array}[]{lll}({{T^{\times}}})^{2}S_{X}&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}{T^{\times}}S(\mathcal{X})\\
&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}}{T^{\times}}S(\mathcal{C})\\
&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}}\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{Y}\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y}).\end{array}

Since
[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
is a groupoid by definition,
\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{Y}\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y})=\mathrm{lim}_{\mathcal{Y}\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y})
, for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}
.
By
7.6
,

\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\cong\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathcal{Y}\in[b]{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{Y})\right)

canonically, and therefore, by the universal property of (co)limits, there is a unique isomorphism

(7.13)

\begin{array}[]{lll}{T^{\times}}^{2}S_{X}&\cong&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\right).\end{array}

∎

By
7.2
, each
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})
inherits the structure of an
X
-graph from
\mathcal{X}
. Therefore, using
7.12
, there is a morphism
\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}S_{X}:({{T^{\times}}})^{2}S_{X}\to{T^{\times}}S_{X}
in
\mathsf{E}
given by

(7.14)

\ {T^{\times}}^{2}S_{X}=\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}\left(\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{(\mathcal{X})}}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))\right)\to\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\in X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}}S(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})={T^{\times}}S_{X}.

This is natural in
X
and in
S
and hence defines a natural transformation
\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}\colon{T^{\times}}^{2}\Rightarrow{T^{\times}}
.

A unit for the endofunctor
{T^{\times}}
on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
is provided by the natural transformation
{\eta^{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}\colon id_{\mathsf{GS}}\Rightarrow{T^{\times}}
induced by the inclusion
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr_{et}}\colon S_{X}\xrightarrow{=}S({\mathcal{C}_{X}})\to{T^{\times}}S_{X}
.

It is straightforward to verify that the triple
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}=({T^{\times}},\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}},\eta^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}})
defines a monad.

7.3.
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
-algebras are non-unital circuit operads.

As usual, let
\mathsf{E}
be an arbitrary category with sufficient limits and colimits.

The purpose of this section is to establish an isomorphism of categories between the category
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}}
of
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
-algebras, and the category
{\mathsf{CO}}^{-}_{\mathsf{E}}
of non-unital circuit operads.

As a first example, let us describe the circuit operad structure on
{T^{\times}}S
where
S
is an arbitrary graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
.

Example 7.15
.

Let
X
and
Y
be finite sets. Given an
X
-graph
\mathcal{X}
and a
Y
-graph
\mathcal{Y}
, the graph
\mathcal{X}\amalg\mathcal{Y}
has the structure of an
X\amalg Y
-graph and is therefore trivially the colimit of a
\mathcal{C}_{X\amalg Y}
-shaped graph of graphs. It is also, by definition, the colimit of a
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}\amalg{\mathcal{C}_{Y}}
-shaped graph of graphs.

Disjoint union of graphs induces a external product
\boxtimes
on
{T^{\times}}S
. Since
\oslash\in\mathbf{0}{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
, there is a morphism
*=S(\oslash)\to{T^{\times}}S_{\mathbf{0}}
from the terminal object
*
in
\mathsf{E}
, and this provides an external unit (see
5.14
) for
\boxtimes
. For all admissible
X
-graphs
\mathcal{X}
and admissible
Y
-graphs
\mathcal{Y}
, the following diagram – in which the unlabelled maps are the defining morphisms for
{T^{\times}}
– commutes in
\mathsf{E}
:

\textstyle{S(\mathcal{X}\amalg\mathcal{Y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Remark 7.19
.

The monad in
[
23
]
is defined identically to
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
on
\mathsf{GS}
except the empty graph
\oslash
is not included as a
\mathbf{0}
-graph. Consequently, algebras for the monad in
[
23
]
are equipped with a non-unital external product. See also
5.15
.

Remark 7.20
.

It is straightforward to verify that
{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}
and
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}\subset\mathsf{GS}
satisfy the conditions of
[
3
, Sections 1 & 2]
and hence there is an abstract nerve theorem, analogous to
10.4
, for
\mathsf{Set}
-valued non-unital circuit operads. This construction is not described here since the nerve theorem for unital circuit operads is far more interesting (and challenging).

8.
Review of modular operads and pointed graphs

This section reviews the construction of the monad for
\mathsf{Set}
-valued modular operads in
[
34
, Sections 5-7]
– in terms of monads
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{D}
on
\mathsf{GS}
and a distributive law
\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}:TD\Rightarrow DT
for composing them – in the more general setting of a category
\mathsf{E}
that is assumed to have sufficient limits and colimits.

8.1.
Non-unital modular operads

The non-unital modular operad monad
\mathbb{T}=(T,\mu^{\mathbb{T}},\eta^{\mathbb{T}})
on
\mathsf{GS}
was described in
[
34
, Section 5]
. In this paper,
\mathbb{T}
is used to denote
the non-unital
\mathsf{E}
-valued modular operad monad on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
where
\mathsf{E}
is an arbitrary enriching category with all finite limits and sufficient colimits.

The endofunctor
\mathbb{T}\colon{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}\to{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
is described, for all graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
, and all finite sets
X
, by

(8.1)

TS_{X}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}S(\mathcal{X}),

where
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}\subset X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
is the full subgroupoid of connected
X
-graphs.

By
[
34
, Corollary 5.19]
(which follows
[
22
, Lemma 1.5.12]
), if
\mathcal{G}
is a connected graph, and
\mathbf{\Gamma}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
is a non-degenerate
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs such that
\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)
is connected for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
, then the colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{G})
is also connected. Hence, the triple
\mathbb{T}=(T,\mu^{\mathbb{T}},\eta^{\mathbb{T}})
, where
\mu^{\mathbb{T}},\eta^{\mathbb{T}}
are the obvious (co)restrictions, defines a monad on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
.

Proposition 8.2
.

The EM category
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}^{\mathbb{T}}
of algebras for
\mathbb{T}
on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
is canonically equivalent to
{\mathsf{MO}}^{-}_{\mathsf{E}}
.

Proof.

The proof for general
\mathsf{E}
closely resembles the proof of
[
34
, Proposition 5.29]
for the case
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Set}
.

The inclusion
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}\hookrightarrow X{\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathrm{iso}}}
induces a monomorphism
TS_{X}\to{T^{\times}}S_{X}
. In particular, since
\mathcal{X}^{x\ddagger y}
is connected for all connected
X
-graphs
\mathcal{X}
and all pairs
x,y
of distinct elements of
X
, if
(A,h)
is an algebra for
\mathbb{T}
, then
A
admits a contraction
\zeta

\zeta_{X}^{x\ddagger y}\colon A_{X}^{x\ddagger y}=A(\mathcal{C}_{X}^{x\ddagger y})\xrightarrow{h}TA_{X\setminus\{x,y\}},

that satisfies (M2) by the proof of
7.16
.

Moreover,
there is a multiplication
\diamond
on
A
such that, for all finite sets
X
and
Y
and all elements
x\in X
, and
y\in Y
,
\diamond_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y}
is induced by

A(\mathcal{D}_{X,Y}^{x\ddagger y})\to TA_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}}\xrightarrow{h}A_{(X\amalg Y)\setminus\{x,y\}}.

To show that
(A,\diamond,\zeta)
satisfy the axioms (M1)-(M4), we construct (as in the proof of
7.16
and
[
34
, Proposition 5.29]
), for each axiom, a pair of graphs of graphs that have the same colimit in
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

For example, to prove Axiom (M4), let
X
and
Y
be finite sets with distinct elements
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
. Then the diagram

\textstyle{(A_{\scriptscriptstyle{X}}\times A_{\scriptscriptstyle{Y}})^{\scriptscriptstyle{x\ddagger y_{1},x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{A_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},y_{1}\}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces A(\mathcal{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle{X,Y}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}})^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{A(\mathcal{C}_{X}\amalg\mathcal{C}_{Y})^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1},x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(\mathcal{D}_{\scriptscriptstyle{X,Y}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}})^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\scriptstyle{\diamond}
\textstyle{A_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{2},y_{2}\}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A(\mathcal{C}_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},y_{1}\}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA(\mathcal{C}_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},y_{1}\}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{2}\ddagger y_{2}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{TA(\mathcal{C}_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{2},y_{2}\}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A(\mathcal{C}_{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{2},y_{2}\}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{x_{1}\ddagger y_{1}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces T^{2}A_{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Th}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}A}}
\textstyle{TA_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces T^{2}A_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{{\mathbb{T}^{\times}}A}}
\scriptstyle{Th}
\textstyle{TA_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{A_{\scriptscriptstyle{X\amalg Y\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\}}}}

commutes, whereby it follows that

\diamond
and
\zeta
satisfy (M4).

Axioms (M1)-(M3) follow by the same method. (Of course, (M2) is proved in
7.16
, and, when
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Set}
, the proof that
(A,h)
satisfies (M1) is described in detail in
[
34
]
.)

The converse follows exactly the same method as the proof of
[
34
, Proposition 5.29]
and
7.16
. ∎

8.2.
The monad
\mathbb{D}

The modular operad monad on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
will be described in terms of a distributive law for composing
\mathbb{T}
and the
pointed graphical species monad

\mathbb{D}=(D,\mu^{\mathbb{D}},\eta^{\mathbb{D}})
. This was defined on graphical species in
\mathsf{Set}
in
[
34
]
, and is generalised here to a monad on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
, for
\mathsf{E}
with sufficient limits and colimits.

The endofunctor
D
adjoins
formal (contracted) units
to a graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
according to
DS_{\lx@sectionsign}=S_{\lx@sectionsign}
with
DS_{\tau}=S_{\tau}
and, for finite sets
X
:

DS_{X}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}S_{X}&X\not\cong\mathbf{2},\text{ and }X\not\cong\mathbf{0},\\
S_{\mathbf{2}}\amalg S_{\lx@sectionsign}&X=\mathbf{2},\\
S_{\mathbf{0}}\amalg\widetilde{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}&X=\mathbf{0},\end{array}\right.

where
\widetilde{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}
is the coequaliser of the identity and
S_{\tau}
on
S_{\lx@sectionsign}

There are canonical natural transformations
\eta^{\mathbb{D}}\colon 1_{{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}}\Rightarrow D
, provided by the induced morphism
S\hookrightarrow DS
, and
\mu^{\mathbb{D}}\colon D^{2}\Rightarrow D
induced by the canonical projections
D^{2}S_{\mathbf{2}}\to DS_{\mathbf{2}}
, so that
\mathbb{D}=(D,\mu^{\mathbb{D}},\eta^{\mathbb{D}})
defines the
pointed graphical species monad
on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
.

Algebras for
\mathbb{D}
– called
pointed graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
– are graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
equipped with
a distinguished unit-like morphism (
5.17
)
\epsilon\colon S_{\lx@sectionsign}\to S_{\mathbf{2}},

and a distinguished morphism
o\colon S_{\lx@sectionsign}\to S_{\mathbf{0}}
that factors through
\widetilde{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}
.

Let
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}
be category
obtained from
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
by formally adjoining morphisms

u\colon\mathbf{2}\to\lx@sectionsign
and
z\colon\mathbf{0}\to\lx@sectionsign
, subject to the relations:

(i)

u\circ ch_{1}=id\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,\lx@sectionsign)
and
u\circ ch_{2}=\tau\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,\lx@sectionsign)
;

(ii)

\tau\circ u=u\circ\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\mathbf{2},\lx@sectionsign)
;

(iii)

z=\tau\circ z\in{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\mathbf{0},\lx@sectionsign)
.

It is straightforward to verify that
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}
is completely described by:

(1)

{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}(\lx@sectionsign,\lx@sectionsign)={{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\lx@sectionsign,\lx@sectionsign)
and
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}(Y,X)={{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(Y,X)
whenever
Y\not\cong\mathbf{0}
and
Y\not\cong\mathbf{2}
;

(2)

{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}(\mathbf{0},\lx@sectionsign)=\{z\}
, and
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}(\mathbf{0},X)={{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\mathbf{0},X)\amalg\{ch_{x}\circ z\}_{x\in X}
;

(3)

{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}(\mathbf{2},\lx@sectionsign)=\{u,\tau\circ u\}
, and
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}(\mathbf{2},X)={{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}(\mathbf{0},X)\amalg\{ch_{x}\circ u,ch_{x}\circ\tau\circ u\}_{x\in X}
.

Hence:

Lemma 8.3
.

The EM category
{\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{\mathsf{E}}}_{*}
of algebras for
\mathbb{D}
is the category of
\mathsf{E}
-presheaves on
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}
. In other words, the following are equivalent:

(1)

S_{*}
is a presheaf on
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}
that restricts to a graphical species
S
on
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}
;

(2)

(S,\epsilon,o)
, with
\epsilon=S_{*}(u)
and
o=S_{*}(z)
is a pointed graphical species.

As a consequence of
8.3
, the notation
S_{*}
and
(S,\epsilon,o)
will be used interchangeably to denote the same pointed graphical species.

8.3.
Pointed graphs

The category
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
of connected graphs and
pointed étale morphisms
, obtained in the bo-ff factorisation of the functor
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}\to\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
is described in detail in
[
34
, Section 7.2]
. Here,
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
is extended to the category
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
of
all
graphs and pointed étale morphisms. In other words,
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
is the category defined by the bo-ff factorisation of the functor
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}\xrightarrow{\Upsilon}\mathsf{GS}\to\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
, as in the following commuting diagram of functors:

(8.4)

\textstyle{{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

f.f.
f.f.
\textstyle{\mathsf{Gr}_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
f.f.
\scriptstyle{{\Upsilon_{*}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\text{forget}^{\mathbb{D}}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{Gr_{et}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Upsilon}
b.o.
\textstyle{\mathsf{GS}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\text{free}^{\mathbb{D}}}

The inclusion
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}\to\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
is fully faithful (by uniqueness of bo-ff factorisation), and the induced nerve
{\Upsilon_{*}}\colon\mathsf{Gr}_{*}\to\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
is fully faithful by construction

Let
\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
be a graph. By
Section

8.2
, for each edge
e\in E
, the
ch_{e}
-coloured unit for
{\Upsilon_{*}}\mathcal{G}
is given by
\epsilon^{\mathcal{G}}_{e}=ch_{e}\circ u\in\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}},\mathcal{G})
, and the corresponding contracted unit is given by
o^{\mathcal{G}}_{\tilde{e}}=ch_{e}\circ z\in\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathcal{G})
.

Since the functor
{\Upsilon_{*}}
embeds
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
as a full subcategory of
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}
, I will write
\mathcal{G}
, rather than
{\Upsilon_{*}}\mathcal{G}
, where there is no risk of confusion. In particular, the element category
\mathsf{el}_{*}{({\Upsilon_{*}}\mathcal{G})}
is denoted simply by
\mathsf{el}_{*}{(\mathcal{G})}
and called the

category of pointed elements of a graph
\mathcal{G}
.

For all pointed graphical species
S_{*}
, the forgetful functor
\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{*}\to\mathsf{GS}
induces an inclusion
\mathsf{el}{(S)}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{el}_{*}{(S_{*})}
, and, for all graphs
\mathcal{G}
, the inclusion
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{el}_{*}{(\mathcal{G})}
is final (see
[
30
, Section IX.3]
) by
[
34
, Lemma 7.8]
. Hence,

(8.5)

\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}_{*}{(\mathcal{G})}}S(\mathcal{C})=\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}}S(\mathcal{C})=S(\mathcal{G})

for all pointed graphical species
S_{*}=(S,\epsilon,o)
in
\mathsf{E}
.

Moreover, by
[
34
, Section 7.3]
, the induced inclusion
{\mathsf{\mathsf{GS}}_{\mathsf{E}}}_{*}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{psh_{\mathsf{E}}}(\mathsf{Gr}_{*})
is fully faithful. (In fact,
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
is equivalent to the category of
\mathsf{E}
-sheaves for the obvious extension of the canonical étale topology on
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
to
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
.)

To better understand morphisms in
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
, let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph and
let
W\subset V_{0}\amalg V_{2}
a subset of isolated and bivalent vertices of
\mathcal{G}
.

The
vertex deletion functor (for
W
)
is the
\mathcal{G}
-shaped graph of graphs
{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
given by

{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}({\mathcal{C}_{X}},b)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(\shortmid)&\text{ if }({\mathcal{C}_{X}},b)\text{ is a {neighbourhood} of }v\in W\ (\text{so }X\cong\mathbf{0}\text{ or }X\cong\mathbf{2}),\\
{\mathcal{C}_{X}}&\text{ otherwise. }\end{array}\right.

By
7.2
, this has a colimit
\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
, and, for all
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}
,
{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}
describes a morphism
\mathcal{C}\to{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}(b)
in
{{\mathbf{B}}}^{\scriptstyle{\bm{\lx@sectionsign}}}_{*}
. Hence,
{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}
induces a morphism
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\in\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W})
.

Definition 8.6
.

The morphism
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\in\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W})
induced by
{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}\colon\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
is called the
vertex deletion morphism corresponding to
W\subset V_{0}\cong V_{2}
.

Example 8.7
.

If
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}
is an isolated vertex
*
and
W=\{*\}
, then
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}
is precisely
z\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\to(\shortmid).

Example 8.8
.

For
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}
and
W=V=\{*\}
,
{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}
is the constant functor
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{G})}\to(\shortmid)
:

(8.9)

\textstyle{(\shortmid)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ch_{1}}
\scriptstyle{id_{(\shortmid)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{(\shortmid)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ch_{2}\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{id_{(\shortmid)}}
\textstyle{(\shortmid)}

and hence
\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}=(\shortmid)
and
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}=u\in\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}},\shortmid)
.

More generally, if
\mathcal{G}={\mathcal{L}^{k}}
, and
W=V
, then
{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{\setminus W}
is also the constant functor to
(\shortmid)
and hence
\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}=(\shortmid)
. The induced morphism is denoted by
u^{k}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\colon\mathcal{L}^{k}\to\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
:

\textstyle{(\shortmid)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ch_{1}}
\scriptstyle{id_{(\shortmid)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
…
\scriptstyle{ch_{2}\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{ch_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\shortmid)}
\scriptstyle{ch_{2}\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{id_{(\shortmid)}}
\textstyle{(\shortmid)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
…
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(\shortmid).}

In particular,
u^{1}=u\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}\to(\shortmid)
and
u^{0}
is just the identity on
(\shortmid)
.

Example 8.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{W}
be the wheel graph with one vertex
v
. Then
\mathcal{W}_{/\{v\}}=\mathrm{colim}_{\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{W})}}{\mathbf{\Lambda}}^{\mathcal{W}}_{/\{v\}}
exists and is isomorphic to
(\shortmid)
in
\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
. The induced morphism
\kappa\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathsf{del}_{\setminus\{v\}}\colon\mathcal{W}\to(\shortmid)
is described in (
8.11
).

Hence, there are precisely two morphisms
\kappa
and
\tau\circ\kappa
in
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\shortmid)
:

(8.11)

\textstyle{\mathcal{W}}
\textstyle{(\shortmid)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ch_{a}}
\scriptstyle{ch_{2}\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{ch_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}}\mapsto a}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{(\shortmid)}

(8.12)

\textstyle{\mathcal{W}}
\textstyle{(\shortmid)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ch_{a}}
\scriptstyle{ch_{2}\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{ch_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}}\mapsto a}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\mathbf{2}}}
\textstyle{(\shortmid)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ch_{1}\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{ch_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{(\shortmid).}

More generally, let
\mathcal{W}^{m}
be the wheel graph with
m
vertices
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
, and let
\iota\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{L}^{m-1},\mathcal{W}^{m})
be an étale inclusion. If
W
is the image of
V(\mathcal{L}^{m-1})
in
V(\mathcal{W}^{m})
, then
V(\mathcal{W}^{m})\cong W\amalg\{*\}
, and by (
8.11
) and
8.8
,
there are two distinct pointed morphisms,
\kappa^{m}
and
\tau\circ\kappa^{m}
, in
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{W}^{m},\shortmid)
. Hence, for all
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{W}^{m},\mathcal{G})=\mathsf{Gr_{et}}(\mathcal{W}^{m},\mathcal{G})\amalg\{ch_{e}\circ\kappa^{m}\}_{e\in E(\mathcal{G})}\cong\mathsf{Gr}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{G}).

Remark 8.13
.

Recall from
3.22
that the composition
\bigcirc=\cup\circ\cap
of Brauer diagrams is not described by a pushout of cospans as in (
3.23
). Instead, the pushout (
3.23
) for
(\cup\circ\cap)

is induced by
the trivial diagram
(\shortmid)\xrightarrow{id}(\shortmid)\xleftarrow{id}(\shortmid)
, and therefore agrees with
\kappa\colon\mathcal{W}\to(\shortmid)
described in
8.10
.

(See
[
34
, Section 6]
for more details on the combinatorics of contractions.)

For convenience, let us define
\mathcal{W}^{0}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}\mathcal{W}^{m}_{/V(\mathcal{W}^{m})}=(\shortmid)
(
m\geq 1
). Then for all
k\geq 0
,
u^{k}\colon\mathcal{L}^{k}\to(\shortmid)
factors canonically as
\mathcal{L}^{k}\to\mathcal{W}^{k}\xrightarrow{\kappa^{k}}(\shortmid)
.

Example 8.14
.

Assume that
\mathcal{G}\not\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}
is connected (so
V_{0}
is empty). Let
W\subset V_{2}
be a subset of bivalent vertices of
\mathcal{G}
. Unless
W=V(\mathcal{G})
and
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{W}^{m}
for some
m\geq 1
, the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}
may be intuitively described as ‘
\mathcal{G}
with a subset of bivalent vertices deleted’ as in
Figure

19
.

In this case, the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}
is described explicitly by:

112.02423pt\raise 6.06001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.24554pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t_{\setminus W}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 150.12502pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern where

\begin{array}[]{ll}V_{\setminus W}&=V\setminus W,\\
H_{\setminus W}&=H\setminus\left(\coprod_{v\in W}\nicefrac{{H}}{{v}}\right),\\
&=E\setminus\left(\coprod_{v\in W}\nicefrac{{E}}{{v}}\right),\end{array}

and
s_{\setminus W},t_{\setminus W}
are just the restrictions of
s
and
t
. The description of the involution
\tau_{\setminus W}
is more complicated than needed here. (The interested reader is referred to
[
34
, Section 7.2]
which contains full descriptions of the vertex deletion morphisms).

\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}

Figure 19.
Vertex deletion
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}
.

Definition 8.15
.

The
similarity category

\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
is the identity-on-objects subcategory of
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
whose morphisms are generated under composition by
z\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\to(\shortmid)
, the vertex deletion morphisms, and graph isomorphisms. Morphisms in
\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
are called
similarity morphisms
, and connected components of
\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
are
similarity classes
. Graphs in the same connected component of
\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
are
similar
.

The following proposition summarises some important basic properties of vertex deletion morphisms. Detailed proofs may be found in
[
34
, Section 7.2]
.

Proposition 8.16
.

For all graphs
\mathcal{G}
and all subsets
W\subset V_{2}
of bivalent vertices of
\mathcal{G}
,

(1)

\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}
preserves connected components, and may be defined componentwise;

(2)

if
\mathcal{G}
is connected and
W=V
, then
\mathcal{G}\cong\mathcal{L}^{k}
or
\mathcal{G}\cong\mathcal{W}^{m}
for
k,m\geq 0
and
\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}\cong(\shortmid)
;

(3)

unless
\mathcal{G}\cong\mathcal{W}^{m}
and
W=V
for
m\geq 1
, if
\mathcal{G}
is connected, then
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}
induces an identity on ports:
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\colon E_{0}(\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{=}E_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W})
.

The pair
(\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}},\mathsf{Gr_{et}})
of subcategories of
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
defines an orthogonal
factorisation system on
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
.

Example 8.17
.

For all graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
, and all graphs
\mathcal{G}
with no isolated vertices,

(8.18)

DS(\mathcal{G})=\coprod_{W\subset V_{2}}S(\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}).

More generally, since
DS_{\mathbf{0}}=DS(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}})=S_{\mathbf{0}}\amalg\widetilde{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}
:

(8.19)

DS(\mathcal{G})\cong\mathrm{colim}_{(\mathcal{H},f)\in\mathcal{G}/\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{sim}}}S(\mathcal{H}),\quad\text{ for all graphs }\mathcal{G}\in\mathsf{Gr_{et}}.

Example 8.20
.

Let
S_{*}=(S,\epsilon,o)
be a pointed graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
. For
k,m\geq 0
,
there are distinguished monomorphisms in
\mathsf{E}
:

(8.21)

S_{*}(u^{k})\colon S_{\lx@sectionsign}\to S(\mathcal{L}^{k}),\ \text{ and }S_{*}(\kappa^{m})\colon S_{\lx@sectionsign}\to S(\mathcal{W}^{m}).

8.4.
The distributive law
TD\Rightarrow DT

For the remainder of this section, all graphs will be assumed to be connected, unless stated otherwise.

Recall from
7.7
that an
X
-graph
\mathcal{X}
is said to be admissible if it has no stick components.
For any finite set
X
, we may enlarge the category
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}
of admissible connected
X
-graphs and port-preserving isomorphisms, to form a category
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
that includes vertex deletion morphisms
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}
and
z\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\to(\shortmid)
out of
X
-graphs together with any labelling of ports from the domain:

By
8.16
, any similarity morphism that is not of the form
z\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\to(\shortmid)
or
\kappa^{m}\colon\mathcal{W}^{m}\to(\shortmid)
is boundary preserving. So, let
\mathcal{X}=(\mathcal{G},\rho)
be an admissible connected
X
-graph such that
\mathcal{X}\not\cong\mathcal{W}^{m}
,
\mathcal{X}\not\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}
, and let
f\in\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime})
be a similarity morphism. Then
f
and the
X
-labelling
\rho\colon E_{0}\to X
for
\mathcal{X}
, induce an
X
-labelling on
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
The category
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
is obtained by adjoining to the category
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}
of (admissible) connected
X
-graphs and port-preserving isomorphisms, all similarity morphisms from objects of
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}
,
and their codomains, equipped with the induced labelling.

•

If
X\not\cong\mathbf{0}
,
X\not\cong\mathbf{2}
, then
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
is the category whose objects are connected
X
-graphs and whose morphisms are similarity morphisms
that preserve the labelling of the ports.

•

For
X=\mathbf{2}
,
\mathbf{2}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
contains the morphisms
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus V}\colon\mathcal{L}^{k}\to(\shortmid)
, and hence the labelled stick graphs
(\shortmid,id)
and
(\shortmid,\tau)
. There are no non-trivial morphisms out of these special graphs, and
\mathcal{X}
is in the same connected component as
(\shortmid,id)
if and only if
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{L}^{k}
(with the identity labelling) for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
\tau\colon(\shortmid)\to(\shortmid)
does not induce a morphism in
\mathbf{2}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
.

•

Finally, when
X=\mathbf{0}
, the morphisms
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus V}\colon\mathcal{W}^{m}\to(\shortmid)
, and
z\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\to(\shortmid)
are not boundary-preserving, and, in particular, do not equip
(\shortmid)
with any labelling of its ports. So, the objects of
\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
are the admissible
\mathbf{0}
-graphs, and
(\shortmid)
.

In particular,
\mathcal{W}^{m},\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}
and
(\shortmid)
are in the same connected component of
\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
. Since
(\shortmid)
is not admissible, there are no non-trivial morphisms in
\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
with
(\shortmid)
as domain.

To simplify notation in what follows, we write
{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}}_{\setminus V}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}(\shortmid)
and
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus V}=z\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\to(\shortmid)
.

The following lemma is immediate from the definitions:

Lemma 8.22
.

For all finite sets
X
and all connected
X
-graphs
\mathcal{X}
, the connected component of
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
containing
\mathcal{X}
has a terminal object
\mathcal{X}^{\bot}
– without bivalent or isolated vertices – given by
\mathcal{X}_{/(V_{2}\amalg V_{0})}
.

If
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathcal{L}^{k}
or
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathcal{W}^{m}
,
\mathcal{X}\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}
for some
k,m\geq 0
, then
\mathcal{X}^{\bot}\cong(\shortmid)
. Otherwise,
\mathcal{X}^{\bot}
is an admissible, connected
X
-graph.

Let
S
be a graphical species in
\mathsf{E}
.
By (
8.19
), if
\mathcal{X}
is a connected
X
-graph and
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}^{\mathcal{X}}
is the connected component of
\mathcal{X}
in
X\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}
, then there is a canonical isomorphism

DS(\mathcal{X})\cong\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr}_{\mathsf{sim}}^{\mathcal{X}}}S(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}).

In particular,
TDS_{X}
is described by a colimit – indexed by pairs
(\mathcal{X},W)
, of a connected
X
-graph
\mathcal{X}
and a subset
W\subset V_{0}\amalg V_{2}
of vertices of
(\mathcal{X})
, and isomorphisms
\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
in
X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}
– of objects
S(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W})
in
\mathsf{E}
. The distributive law
\lambda_{\mathbb{D},\mathbb{T}}\colon TD\Rightarrow DT
is induced by forgetting the pair
(\mathcal{X},W)
, and remembering only
\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W}
, in the evaluation of
S(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W})
.

Precisely, for
X\not\cong\mathbf{0}
,

TDS_{X}=\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}DS(\mathcal{X})=\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}\coprod_{W\subset V_{2}(\mathcal{X})}S(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W}).

Since vertex deletion morphisms preserve ports when
X\neq\mathbf{0}
, each
\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W}
is either an admissible
X
-graph or
\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W}\cong(\shortmid)
, and hence there are canonical morphisms

TDS_{X}\to\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}S(\mathcal{X})&=TS_{X}&=DTS_{X}&\text{ when }X\not\cong\mathbf{0},X\not\cong\mathbf{2},\\
\left(\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}S(\mathcal{X})\right)\amalg S_{\lx@sectionsign}&=TS_{\mathbf{2}}\amalg S_{\lx@sectionsign}&=DTS_{\mathbf{2}}&\text{ when }X=\mathbf{2}\end{array}\right.

(since
(\shortmid)
is not a
\mathbf{2}
-graph by convention). These describe the morphisms
\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}S_{X}\colon TDS_{X}\to DTS_{X}
in
\mathsf{E}
for
X\not\cong\mathbf{0}
.

When
X=\mathbf{0}
,

\begin{array}[]{lll}TDS_{\mathbf{0}}&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}DS(\mathcal{X})\\
&=&\left(\mathrm{colim}_{\overset{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}{\mathcal{X}\not\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}}}\coprod_{W\subset V_{2}(\mathcal{X})}S(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W})\right)\amalg DS(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}})\\
&=&\left(\mathrm{colim}_{\overset{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}{\mathcal{X}\not\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}}}\coprod_{W\subset V_{2}(\mathcal{X})}S(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W})\right)\amalg S_{\mathbf{0}}\amalg\widetilde{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}.\end{array}

Vertex deletion maps
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}_{\setminus W}
from
\mathbf{0}
-graphs preserve ports unless
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\cong z\colon\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}\to(\shortmid)
or
\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\cong\kappa^{m}\colon\mathcal{W}^{m}\to(\shortmid)
. So,
\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}S_{\mathbf{0}}\colon TDS_{\mathbf{0}}\to DTS_{\mathbf{0}}
is given by the canonical morphism

TDS_{\mathbf{0}}=\left(\mathrm{colim}_{\overset{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}{\mathcal{X}\not\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}}}\coprod_{W\subset V_{2}(\mathcal{X})}S(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W})\right)\amalg S_{\mathbf{0}}\amalg\widetilde{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}\to\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbf{0}\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}S(\mathcal{X})\amalg\widetilde{S_{\lx@sectionsign}}=DTS_{\mathbf{0}}.

The verification that
\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}
satisfies the four distributive law axioms
[
2
]
follows by a straightforward application of the definitions. To help the reader gain familiarity with the constructions, I describe just one here, namely that the following diagram of endofunctors on
{\mathsf{GS}_{\mathsf{E}}}
commutes:

(8.23)

\textstyle{T^{2}D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{\mathbb{T}}D}
\textstyle{TDT\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}T}
\textstyle{DT^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D\mu^{\mathbb{T}}}
\textstyle{TD\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}}
\textstyle{DT.}

Recall, from (
7.14
) and (
8.18
), that, for all graphical species
S
in
\mathsf{E}
and all finite sets
X
,

\begin{array}[]{lll}T^{2}DS_{X}&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}\mathrm{lim}_{(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}}\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{Y}^{b}\in[b]\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}\coprod_{W^{b}\subset V_{2}}S(\mathcal{Y}^{b}_{\setminus W^{b}})\\
&=&\mathrm{colim}_{\mathcal{X}\in X\mathchar 45\relax\mathsf{CGr_{iso}}}\mathrm{lim}_{\mathbf{\Gamma}\in\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}^{(\mathcal{X})}}\coprod_{W^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\subset V_{2}(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))}S(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})_{\setminus W^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}),\end{array}

where, as usual,
\iota_{{v}}\colon{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{v}}}\to\mathcal{X}
is the essential morphism. So, to describe the two paths in (
8.23
),
let
\mathcal{X}
be a connected
X
-graph,
\mathbf{\Gamma}
a non-degenerate
\mathcal{X}
-shaped graph of connected graphs, and let
W^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\subset(V_{0}\amalg V_{2})(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))
be a subset of (bivalent or isolated) vertices of the colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})
of
\mathbf{\Gamma}
in
\mathsf{C}\mathsf{Gr_{et}}
.

For each
(\mathcal{C},b)\in\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}
, let

W^{b}\overset{\textup{def}}{=}V(\mathbf{\Gamma}(b))\cap W^{\mathbf{\Gamma}},\ \text{ so }W^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\cong\coprod_{v\in V(\mathcal{X})}W^{\iota_{{v}}}.

If
(\mathcal{X},\mathbf{\Gamma},W)=(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathbf{I}^{\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}},V(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}))
, then
\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}}
, and
(\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{0}},b)
is the unique object of
\mathsf{el}{(\mathcal{X})}
.

Otherwise, since
\mathcal{X}
is connected, it must be the case that
W^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}\subset V_{2}(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X}))
.

In each case we may define
W\subset V(\mathcal{X})
to be the set of vertices
v
of
\mathcal{X}
such that
W^{\iota_{{v}}}=V(\mathbf{\Gamma}(\iota_{{v}}))
.
Then,
there is a unique non-degenerate
\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W}
-shaped graph of connected graphs
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\setminus W}
given by
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\setminus W}(\mathsf{del}_{\setminus W}\circ b)=\mathbf{\Gamma}(b)_{\setminus W^{b}}
, and with colimit
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\setminus W}(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W})
in
\mathsf{Gr}_{*}
:

(8.24)

\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\setminus W}(\mathcal{X}_{\setminus W})=\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathcal{X})_{\setminus W^{\mathbf{\Gamma}}}.

T\lambda_{\mathbb{D}\mathbb{T}}

These are equal since
